ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e300	It is true, as the President says, that there are several issues, but I will focus on the necessary legislative changes that are linked to government proposals and that are dealt with in the Storting in the context of the revised national budget. The opinion also deals with a number of other proposals from the minority, which I believe they will explain themselves. The vast majority of changes in the field of taxation proposed by the government have the full agreement of the Committee, but in a few areas there is disagreement. I want to say something about these in the following. One of the issues of conflict in the setting concerns the petroleum tax and restrictions on the so-called free income. As you know, the government has proposed to reduce the rate of exemption from 7,5 pst. to 5.5 pst. As is known in practice, the exemption is an additional favourable depreciation option for investments on the base. This proposal thus reduces the total revenue from 30 pst. until 22 pst. The resources on Norwegian pedestal belong to the people of Norway. Petroleum taxes shall ensure that, when these values are taken up, the rent on land – the extraordinary return – is due to the owner, and thus the owner is the Norwegian people, the Norwegian community. At the same time, it's important to have a system that facilitates, so that we get investments where resources are actually taken up. These considerations — i.e. an investment-friendly tax system on the one hand, but, at the same time, a tax system where the rent on land belongs to the community — are not as many countries that have managed to combine just as much as we do here in Norway. But we've done that, and the moderate tightening of the income, which the majority of the committee assumes the Storting does not change this picture. In our view, this is a minor adjustment to a bias that has caused the state to cover over time too much of the investment costs on the base. After the proposal, companies must take a slightly higher proportion of the investment costs. Stability and foreseeable framework conditions are, and have been, a strength for the Norwegian petroleum tax system. The main features of this system have been fixed since 1992 and continue to do so. I want to add that this stability is very important. But stability itself cannot stand in the way of change – in the light of developments in the sector and improvements in the system. Even after this moderate, cautious approach, which there is reason to believe gets a majority – I'm going to get back to it – there will still be a very investment-friendly tax regime on Norwegian pedestal. The proposal now to be implemented is well known, discussed and analysed by several governments, but it is only now that it is implemented. The majority of the Committee therefore considers that the change over time, together with the growth package proposed by the government for the mainland, will contribute to increased cost awareness in industry and that it will also contribute over time to a more balanced development of the Norwegian economy. I have noticed through the hearing, in the media and through the suspension we are now debating, that there seems to be broad support for the measures proposed. The fact that all lots, with the exception of the Advance Party, are behind the proposal creates predictability and security around the petroleum tax system. That's good and important. Finally, I would like to add some comments to another proposal on which there is disagreement. It is a matter of exemption from the professional secrecy of lawyers in the field of taxation. The majority of the Committee supports the proposed amendment to the rules and justifies this with the importance of preventing taxable persons and companies from being controlled by transferring funds or keeping them in accounts of a lawyer with professional secrecy. In order to determine the correct tax and levy, it is central that the tax authorities are provided with information on money transactions and transfers of funds. The authorities must be provided with information about who is behind them and who owns the amounts that are transferred. The majority of the Committee therefore agrees that lawyers and other third parties who have such information should provide that information to the tax authorities when they so require, without prejudice to the obligation of professional secrecy. This is not a consensus. I assume that the opposition will explain its opposition to the proposal later on. With this I recommend the setting in the two mentioned cases.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e326	The attitudes we deal with affect a number of tax matters. I'm not going to discuss everyone, but I'm going to go into some of them in detail. The Progress Party agrees with the government that the community owns the resources on Norwegian pedestal and that revenue is important for the long-term financing of the welfare society. That long-term well-being despite these revenues is not sustainable is a discussion we will return to many times in the years that come. However, although the resources on the Norwegian base are important for the community, there is no direct correlation between increased tax conditions and increased tax revenues. The Progress Party notes that the government believes that the tax tightening introduced for the free income of the petroleum tax will increase the tax revenue from the petroleum industry by 70 billion. kr in the years 2013-2050. Income calculations 37 years in advance should be careful. It's a plan economy. The government describes the tax austerity almost as a gift package with no negative ringing effects: 70 billion. kr in without consequences for the industry? Nope. Moderate tightening, to which the chairman of the committee referred? Nope. The aim of the exemption is to cover investments with normal profitability from special taxes. The tax change will reduce the profitability of future projects and will affect marginal fields. This will affect activity levels for many years to come in time both in terms of tail production and increased extraction. 2 billion. kr in the year of increased tax will be marked. What about the stability of the tax system? I note that the committee leader pointed out that it was stability, but I suppose you can't change the regime and say that it's stability all the time. That's change, and that's something that the industry will notice. Maybe you should do a professional investigation before making that kind of change. In a majority comment, the government parties comment that the tax will contribute to a more balanced development of the Norwegian economy. But is that an independent objective? What, then, is the point of the Minister of Industry’s areas of activity — which means exactly the opposite of a balanced development in the Norwegian economy? Or does the government have a different definition of what balanced development in the Norwegian economy is? Norway is a small country, and we don't have the conditions to become world champions in total. Our young knowledge talents need to be integrated into industries that develop advanced solutions and new technologies — and revenues. Petroleum industry is doing just that. The government proposes to amend the law so that lawyers can provide the tax authorities with information about their clients’ transfers of funds, deposits and debts and who are party to transfers. The Progress Party agrees that tax authorities should have transparency, but we are sceptical that lawyers are required to break their professional secrecy – a scepticism we share with many authorities. The professional secrecy of lawyers should not be impaired ‘for the greater good’. In January, a selection was set up with a mandate to make a broad assessment of the professional secrecy of lawyers. The Progress Party believes that we should let the sample work out before concluding. The Progress Party opposes the government's proposals and, together with the Right, Christian People's Party and the Left, promotes an alternative where we ask the government to come forward with «proposals whereby the client accounts of lawyers are handled in line with the proposal of the Association and so that exemptions from the obligation of professional secrecy are not necessary». The Progress Party promotes a number of tax proposals in our alternative state budget. We are not going to re-fight these in the revision of the budget, but we are going to have to add them to the state budget for 2014 and 2015. Most people will notice that instead of a red-green government with bans, restrictions, injunctions, regulations, fees and penalties, a new era of positive instruments and tax incentives will be launched that will encourage desired behaviour in a number of areas. Tax exemptions for collective cards and training cards, removal of inheritance fees, individual pension savings and annual savings of NOK 40000, improved BSU scheme, improved wreckage arrangements, better conditions for the tax FUNN, removal of employers’ fees for apprentices, to name a few. In this way, people will be encouraged to take responsibility for their own life situation instead of receiving passive public benefits. In terms of tax FUNN, we propose to increase the own R & D limit to 8 million. SEK and external R & D to SEK 12 million. kr. We will remove the maximum hourly rate rule of 530 kroner and remove the rules that the costs of staff and indirect costs for the number of hours for own employees are limited to a maximum of 1850 hours per year. Furthermore, we want to establish a system that indexes the amounts of own R & D carried out and purchased in the SkattenFUNN scheme each year. We will build up under the will by introducing tax incentives that make volunteering less dependent on public transfers. We therefore propose, together with the other opposition parties, to increase tax deductions for gifts to non-governmental organisations to NOK 25 000 for individuals and DKK 100000 for businesses. Furthermore, we propose that so-called masterless inheritances be extended to non-governmental organisations. I see that time is running out of me. Then I will conclude here and address all the proposals that the Progress Party is promoting alone or with others.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e458	The starting point for the Left's tax policy is that working, saving, investing in Norwegian enterprises and jobs should be profitable, and not least contributing to environmental and climate-friendly societal development. Therefore, the core of the Left's tax policy is a shift from red to green tax. With the tax policy of the Left, working and creating values will be more profitable, living environmentally friendly, but pollution will be less profitable. The left wants to facilitate an innovative business, where innovation and development in existing enterprises are important, and how many start their own business. The left will therefore facilitate better relationships for individual enterprises, not least for better and equal social rights. Various government councils, at the request of the Left, have repeatedly said that they will consider strengthening the social rights of self-employed people. Nothing has happened so far. It is therefore important for the more than 230 000 self-employed that there be a government shift to the autumn. The left today promotes a number of proposals that will improve the conditions of self-employed workers; from better social rights, including sick pay and increased maximum savings in the occupational pension scheme, to better and more sensible rules on the payment of taxes. While the Left will improve business conditions, we will stimulate business to become more climate-friendly and environmentally friendly. Through the tax system, it can be done in two ways: either by increasing environmental taxes or by introducing differentiated rates of premium for desired environmental and climate-friendly behaviour. The left therefore proposes to introduce its own environmental depreciation rate in balancing group d, so that a further 5 pst depreciation is possible. if the investment clearly generates an environmental and/or climate benefit. The same approach is followed by the Left in the case of the TAX FUNN scheme. Today it's only 10 pst. of the research activity within the TaxFUNN that can be related to climate, environmental and efficiency gains. This will increase Left and therefore proposes the introduction of own financial limits in addition to such R & D activities, by 2 million, respectively. NOK 4 million. kr, depending on whether the research has been carried out internally in the enterprise or externally. The same applies to the Left’s proposal today to introduce tax exemptions for paid monthly employer cards on public transport and tax credits for energy savings measures in owner-occupied dwellings. The left considers it important to stimulate saving. Therefore, we propose to increase the amount of the share purchase free in our own company, strengthen the BSU scheme and the individual pension saving scheme. We also propose to abolish the inheritance tax, to remove the employer’s fee for apprentices and to grant employers’ tax exemptions for start-ups with up to five employees in the first three years. On Tuesday of last week, we celebrated the 100th anniversary of women's voting rights. It was an important milestone for equality in Norway. But that doesn't mean that we have real equality in all areas. One of the areas where there is no real equality is in the workplace. There are unacceptable pay differentials between women and men, and many employers employ men rather than women because many women have higher sick leave associated with, among other things. pregnancy. The left therefore proposes that the law on social protection be amended to cover all costs related to sick leave during pregnancy. In this way, a barrier to real equality in work disappears. Unfortunately, over the past few weeks we have seen that the government and government parties are on the wane in terms of privacy and legal certainty, among other things. today with the proposal to exempt lawyers from the obligation of professional secrecy in the field of taxation. As the Left sees it, this is an unnecessary proposal which is also strongly critical of the Riksadvokat. Both the Advance Party, the Right Party and the Christian People's Party have given good reasons why we are voting against this today, so it is unnecessary for me to repeat it. Finally, I refer to the Left's proposal in the setting.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e480	Today, the G8 starts in Belfast. One of the issues on the agenda is taxation and the discussion we are now seeing internationally, which is about protecting the tax base and trying to avoid tax transfers between high and low tax countries. In addition, the issue of information on tax matters is also on the agenda, not least through the work done by the OECD. The size is also today invited to take decisions on a tax agreement with the US, the FATCA agreement, which is about automatic exchange of information between US and Norwegian tax authorities. This is a kind of agreement that is likely to form a template for what we will look at in the international tax area in the future. So these are important areas. That is why there is also an important proposal on the table in what the Storting is discussing today — not only the issue of the US-Norway tax agreement, but also the tax exchange that is taking place by proposing reductions in the deductions for, among others, the tax exchange. multinational companies in terms of keeping revenue where there are low tax rates and expenditure where there are high tax rates. On this basis, the Government proposes to reduce the corporate tax rate by 1 percentage point from 1 January 2014. This should include, among other things: – and mainly – is covered by this tax exchange between tightening deductions and extending the tax base. This is a way of thinking about taxes that are well in line with the basic principles of the Norwegian tax debate. Then the Scheel Committee will continue to work on the problem until autumn 2014. Let me say a few words about the oil tax, in addition to what the case-rapporteur was about. I agree that stability and predictability are important for companies that make large investments. As a result, the main features of the oil tax system have also been fixed since the 1992 tax reform. Nevertheless, a implemented adjustment over time has been made – among others. the tax system on the base has gradually evolved into a system based on net income. It is also important to note that, unlike in many other countries, Norway has not increased the oil tax conditions in the last decade when we have had a strongly rising oil price. At the same time, the argument on stability is not an argument against making adjustments or changes. The most important thing is that any change is predictable on the basis of a general principle, which we have been clear about, and this general principle is that the rent is largely due to the person who owns the resource, namely the Norwegian people. It is also the case that the weakness of the oil tax system has been discussed on various occasions, including: in the follow-up to the 2001 oil tax sample and in the follow-up to the so-called KonKraft report of the revised national budget in 2004. So the companies can't have what you can call a 'corrected expectation' that distortions in a tax system will persist. Moreover, making some adjustments to the oil tax system is not the main cause of the fundamental stability of oil companies – the large fluctuations in the oil price show that this is a sector that is accustomed to major and unpredictable changes. But as I said, the changes we are now making in the oil tax system are, in my view, adjustments and do not reflect the fundamental stability of this system. Finally, let me just say two words about Document 8-110 S, about the compensation scheme. That scheme is there today to ensure that the choice of local authorities for self-production or output should not be affected by the added-value system. This is not a support scheme, but a scheme covered by the income system for municipalities. I would also like to show that the House Bank has quite extensive support arrangements for the adaptation of own-dwelling.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e639	I would like to comment on the proposal for VAT compensation in connection with the construction of nursing homes, which has been discussed by both the Minister of Finance and several others. The purpose of the general compensation scheme for the local government sector is to neutralise the distortions of competition that VAT may give rise to when municipalities are faced with the choice of providing taxable services themselves or purchasing services from private enterprises. The scheme is financed by the municipalities themselves in terms of the state transfers corresponding to the extent of the scheme when it was introduced, so that it is thus non-profit-making. The proposal for a representative proposes, in a simple summary, that private individuals building nursing homes for their children with developmental disabilities be covered by the VAT compensation system. This is about a group we must absolutely bear in mind when developing policies in different areas. A loan and grant scheme has been developed, including: for several disabled persons to have a financial opportunity to live in owner-occupied dwellings. In particular, grants are granted on the basis of gross capital formation, which means that VAT is taken into account in the measurement of subsidies. I would like to commend the proposers for putting further search lights in this area. However, there are several reasons why the majority does not support the adoption of the proposal. Firstly, an extension of the scheme will break for its fundamental purpose. We therefore believe that this is an area that deserves careful evaluation before any decision on changes is taken. Secondly, the limits of the compensation scheme have been discussed. The Supreme Court has ordered two judges, and the Riksrevision has examined payments made in error. The majority consider it important to clarify who is eligible for compensation, so that the VAT compensation scheme can work after the end of the year. The Ministry of Finance has stated that there may be a need to assess some clarifications and any changes to make the legislation clearer and more user-friendly. This includes, inter alia: to further assess the design of the regulatory framework with regard to the right of compensation of private and non-profit institutions serving households for the construction of residential housing. The Ministry states that it will assess these issues more closely and possibly come back with concrete legislative proposals at a later stage, after the matter has been discussed. The majority of the Committee shall attend to a review of the status of the national budget for 2014. In this way, this area will be able to be followed up further politically. In the preamble, the majority point out that the Norwegian Association of Developmentally Disabled Persons has proposed that the Compensation Act should be amended in such a way that compensation for the purchase of housing for health or social purposes intended to be permanent homes is no longer granted. The reason given by the NFU is that the compensation scheme contributes to an overinvestment in an institution-like complex, while it is a political objective that people with developmental disabilities should have as much ordinary homes as possible in a normal population. The majority ask the Ministry of Finance to consider the NFU input and whether the limitations of the compensation mechanism in this area are appropriate for the purpose.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e886	The size now has important and absolutely necessary changes to the Patient Rights Act. Five main changes are proposed, which are unanimous. The first proposal – and perhaps the most important – is that healthcare businesses are now responsible for notifying the Health Economics Administration, HELFO, if they are unable to meet the medically prescribed treatment deadline for patients, and not as today, that the patient himself is responsible for contacting HELFO. Today’s legislation means that probably the most resource-intensive patients are found in a complex system. That is why we also see that a very small proportion of patients contact the health economy administration when the deadline is broken. The responsibility for contacting health professionals — not least that they are responsible for making immediate contact — when they see that they do not have the opportunity, is likely to result in more patients being granted their right to study or treatment within the time limit set. The second, which is a proposal that will also mean a great deal to patients, is that, in the first response to referral, health professionals are given responsibility for giving the date and hour of examination or treatment to those who are given a period of time within four months. Those who are granted a period beyond four months shall also be allowed a period, but within a weekly period. This will lead to very many patients experiencing the answer they get back, as much more concrete and understandable, and that many will settle for it because you have a disease or suffering that you can wait for, until the time you've got, as long as you've got a concrete date and hour. Not least, this will require health professionals to have a better overview of their own time use and hours off. The proposal also provides for a medical deadline for all persons entitled to health care and for only two groups to operate: those entitled to health care and those not entitled to health care. The proposal also means that the current 30-day assessment deadline is changed to a 10-day deadline for the assessment of referrals. It is also essentially the reality and practice of today's system. Furthermore, radiology by private X-ray institutes having contracts with health care establishments is covered by the free choice of hospitals. All of these proposals involve in some ways an improvement in patients’ rights and are something to which the Storting unanimously subscribes. I believe the two most important proposals here – that health companies are responsible for contacting HELFO, and that the date and hour of the first enquiry – are the proposals that are most important. These are also proposals that the Right has made as a representative proposal earlier in this period. At that time, they received support from the entire opposition, but they were dissented, but now they have unanimous support. In addition, the opposition promotes three other proposals that should also have been adopted today, but which unfortunately are not adopted. The first proposal implies that rehabilitation, together with X-rays, had already been lifted into the free choice of hospitals. It was part of the draft consultation on this matter and received broad agreement, and the opposition believes that the law amendment could have been implemented already today. Then one could have investigated the practical consequences of the legislative change in the same way as the other legislative changes we are making here, and had a date of entry into force at the same time with – or possibly later than – the others. The second thing we are proposing is that the national lead-times, which are medically justified in the case of cancer, should also be made legally binding. Thus health businesses were also responsible for notifying HELFO if they were unable to follow the lead-times, or had a medical justification that it was not necessary to follow the lead-times. Last but not least, the opposition suggests that it should not only be the duty of health professionals to establish a patient coordinator; the individual patient should also be entitled to do so. It is a matter that has also been raised in the media lately, and it has received broad support, among other things. from all patient and user agents and from Per Fugelli in the evening post last week. Then we also deal with a proposal from the Progress Party to introduce the Danish ‘package course’ into cancer care. It also receives support from the opposition. I refer to the suggestions that the Right promotes with others in the matter.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e912	The thing we're dealing with now is a change in laws that will mean too many people. Today, you wait up to 30 days before you get a response to the referral, and then wait even longer before you get the time for treatment. If you are not eligible, you may not get any specific time then either. The change in laws we're now adopting will change this system. Some hospitals are already in progress, and at the Hospital Østfold they have adopted a goal of giving people time for treatment in the letter of reply to the reference. They're not quite there yet, but they're on the right track. Granting rights is great for those who are covered by the right, but it can lead to it being at the expense of others. That's why I'm glad we're now removing the term "right spaced". Today, some patients are assessed to have the right to the required specialist health care and others only to need health care, while a third group is rejected or referred back to municipal health care. This distinction between rights and non-rights spaces is difficult to understand and practice for both health professionals and patients. Now you either need specialist health care, or you don't want it. If you need to, you will be required to review your referral on an ongoing basis and within 10 working days at the latest. In addition, you are required to have a time to start healthcare within another 10 working days. This applies to all those in need of specialist health care – not just a group. It is very positive that health businesses now have to report to HELFO themselves when they are unable to keep the deadline for treatment. It is good in several ways, and I think it will have a greater impact on health businesses when they themselves have to report. I also believe that many more deadlines will be notified to HELFO. Today, it is the case that the individual patient has to do it himself, and many people do it hard enough to do it. It is important to point out that although health firms are now required to do so, patients can of course continue to contact HELFO themselves. The Committee refers in the preamble to the Special Health Service Act § 2-5a on Patient Coordinator. The Committee is concerned that this legal provision is not adequately followed up by the specialist healthcare service. The majority of the Committee therefore considers that there is a need to assess the wording of the provision in order to ensure that patients’ right to follow up and coordinate their services is respected.

The second guarantee applies to other patients who are guaranteed to start treatment within two months. If this deadline is not met, patients are offered treatment at other public or private hospitals. In addition, a new guarantee for one month of pilot studies and diagnosis is introduced in Denmark during these days. The Progress Party believes it is high time to grant Norwegian patients similar rights to those enjoyed in Denmark. ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e957	The representative Per Arne Olsen has taken up the proposal he referred to.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e960	We have a law-based health system. Then one of the most important tasks of the Stortinget will be to ensure the right to health care for all, in line with the legislator's intention. The legislator's intention must be to give priority to patients who have the principle that those who need the most help should get it first. A too complex piece of legislation can contribute to a red tape that works against the intention to strengthen patient rights. Laws must never be so complicated that they stand in the way of wise health assessments. There is no reference point from other countries to the fact that unit-price financing, privatisation and market alignment of healthcare provide more proper priorities, equal access to public health care or better financial management, rather the opposite. Nevertheless, I note that it is the opposition's response to almost all of the health challenges. Today’s amendment strengthens patient rights in relation to the right to be assessed, including in the event of a delay. The fact that from now on there should be a specialist service and not the patient himself who alerts HELFO about a deadline break is a change that has been called for. It also strengthens patient rights that the time limit for the right to review is changed from 30 to 10 days. In case of serious or life-threatening illness, the patient is entitled to faster assessment and emergency assistance. Elimination of the distinction between patients with rights and patients without rights will hopefully be a positive contribution to less bureaucracy and more effective patient management. When the Storting introduces new rights, the effect must always be carefully followed in order to be able to adjust practices if the change has unintended consequences. In the case of a right of assessment, it is important that the focus on the specialist services is followed by a corresponding focus on the general practitioner’s competence to detect as soon as possible the symptoms that require referral. The European Court of Justice has established that healthcare is covered by the rules on the free movement of services laid down in the EFTA Treaty, on which the Patient Rights Directive is based. The right to hospital treatment in other countries, covered by the social security system in the home country, will affect the health system. We don't know how yet. When treating patients in a country other than where patients have their other responsibilities and rights as citizens, the need to categorize and price different patient groups and treatments increases. The manager must secure the correct price for the service performed, a fair trade. The Patient Rights Directive is the result of a long process in the EU system, where drivers have not been the people or nations but the EU system. In the negotiations, Member States were concerned about the need for planning, cost control and priority countries. In Norway, the Patient Rights Directive is a good example of how the EEA Agreement works in Norwegian democracy. It has not been a popular requirement to become part of a common European health system, and no political party has had this as a battle cause, at least not within today's political plurality. Nevertheless, the directive becomes Norwegian law today. Compared to current law, the Patient Rights Directive extends cross-border healthcare to include hospital treatment. This is according to the Ministry of Regulatory Enforcement of the Court of Justice of the European Union its practice, which the Ministry claims is binding on Norway. So the European Court of Justice will go first, then the elected national parliaments will adopt the law's law-making practice afterwards. The Directive provides that patients should be entitled to health care in all countries that will benefit from the internal market in the EU. Patients should be entitled to reimbursement up to the cost of treatment in their home country. Member States may require prior authorisation for reimbursement of certain health services. If the waiting time in the home country is too long, such pre-approval must be granted. In principle, national legislation is subject to EU law in all areas covered by the decisions on the free movement of services. While a territory is being opened, legal developments in this area will be determined by the Court of Justice of the European Union. Today’s Norwegian interpretation of the Directive will therefore be able to be corrected by decisions of the European Court of Justice. This Directive allows national pre-approval, but does not require it. Reimbursement schemes and large distances may challenge non-discrimination as an important value in health services. The possibility of changes in national health provision to own populations resulting from the harmonisation of countries with very different payment capacities has been little mentioned. It is a long tradition for countries to cooperate in cross-border healthcare. Of course, it is positive when a country’s specialities are made available to patient groups in other countries. It is uncertain what kind of effects it will have if the extent of health travel increases considerably for treatments where the tradition today is close between the patient’s geographical affiliation and the treatment provider.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e988	Changes in the Patient and User Rights Act are important changes that are also in line with some of today’s practices. The Christian People’s Party supports the four main changes – the right to be assessed after referral to the specialist healthcare service, the need to change from 30 to 10 days, the removal of the distinction between rights and non-righteous spaces, the responsibility of the specialist health service to contact HELFO in the event of failure to comply with the deadline for healthcare and the necessary laws to implement the EU Patient Rights Directive. On the part of the Christian People's Party, I would like to underline some additional points. In the current law, the patient’s health status is to be assessed within 30 working days, while the referral is now. This is a change in law. If this is to be seen as a reinforcement for the individual patient, the reference is alpha and omega. Good referrals are not only important to assess the patient early, but it is also important to initiate a good and prudent patient course. The proposal points out that work is being undertaken on a project for the development of a national referral guide. This must be a project at express speed. I ask the State Council to explain this in more detail in its article, how this project is supposed to take care of patients, because a poor reference to hospitals means that someone else has to do the job. It's unnecessary, but you can live with it. What you can't live with is that a lack of references means that the patient – the one referred to – does not get the help it needs. Furthermore, the proposition makes a distinction between the unexplained and the clear state of the patient. In patients who have resolved their condition, the individual legal period shall apply to treatment, but the patient has an undetermined condition, the time limit shall apply to the investigation. For the Christian People's Party, it is important to stress that there must not be a slip from what we today call the clarified state and thus the right to treatment, to the unsolved states, which only give the right to investigation. It's important that the Council of State is concerned. In addition to this, I support the comments made by the rapporteur, the representative of Höie, in his statement on patients' rights, and that they can choose a rehabilitation site, as far as this is concerned, with the lead time for patient treatment, and this with the individual right to a patient coordinator. So to the representative proposal of the Progress Party on a ‘package trajectory’ in cancer care. There is a lot of good in Norwegian cancer care. It is well anchored in research as the starting point for a clinically good treatment. But we must never forget, despite doing a lot of good, that getting a cancer diagnosis is a heavy burden on mental health, but also on treatment. It is therefore important to facilitate good treatment and treatment processes. It is important for me to stress here that information to the patient who is affected, and relatives around is also important. Therefore, the Christian People's Party supports the proposal of the Progress Party for a ‘package course’ for cancer patients.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e1005	The main objective of the legislative changes, which we broadly agree on today – and I agree with the representative Olsen that we have very similar motives here – is to simplify and improve legislation and ensure better and more orderly access to specialist healthcare for patients who need it. The content of the proposals is reproduced by several speakers, so I'm not going to go into it in detail, but I think it's a development that's right. I am pleased that an overall committee is joining the proposals for changes that the government has promoted in the proposition. There are important changes that will help to ensure that patients have good and sound patient profiles. I would also like to emphasise the fact that hospitals are now responsible for taking action if it carries on a deadline breach. It is to provide equivalent services, because it is often the strengths of resources that have been most easily seen to carry that path, while others who do not have insight and resources can remain in a difficult situation. I would like to stress that we do not reach our objectives alone by making changes to the legislation. I'm a little worried that there might be a trend during this day that we think that just we're discussing rights fixing, lawmaking and formulating texts, the reality changes in the clinical everyday life. No, this is a broad development towards better communication, clarification of rights and better practices. This must be done every day in the hospitals. They need to work on empowerment, and patients’ administrative systems and better information for patients need to be put in place in order to comply with the new legislative proposals. The promises we make in ICT will help do this. The government, I can promise, will help to enable hospitals to achieve the goals. Some have commented on the proposal to change the assessment deadline. Among other things, the Cancer Association has been concerned that serious illnesses can be neglected as a result of the proposals. I would like to stress that this proposal is not about weakening the rights of those who are seriously ill – on the contrary. The laws shall apply to all patients in need of health care, both those with cancer and those with conditions that do not require equally rapid treatment. The new rules shall act as a safety net for all patients. Of course, those who become acutely ill or who suspect a serious illness must be assessed and treated more quickly than those with less severe conditions. I agree with the representatives of the Progress Party that cancer patients must be guaranteed healthy, effective and good treatment. At the same time, I would like to stress that we have good quality in Norwegian cancer care today, which means that more and more people survive their cancer. We know the statistics about that. Proper and rapid diagnostics and investigation provide the basis for good treatment outcomes. So we have an ambitious goal, set in dialogue with the trade unions, of that 80 pst. patients must have started treatment within 20 working days. We're moving towards that goal, and we're making progress. The government’s new cancer strategy, presented two weeks ago, is drawing up national objectives in several key areas. An important objective is to achieve well-coordinated and predictable patient pathways. It shall include, inter alia: is done by establishing standard patient profiles in all the action programmes and by further developing the national action programmes to cover the entire patient cycle. Well-organised patient courses with recommended lead-times shall ensure a rapid, predictable and holistic course through diagnostics, investigation, treatment and follow-up. In terms of content, standardised patient profiles will have clear similarities with the Danish model of ‘package trajectory’ for cancer treatment, adapted to the organisation of our healthcare. When it comes to the issue of law-enforcement of lead times, I believe that this is not the way to go. The individual patient must be individually assessed according to the severity of the disease and the urgency of the disease. I believe it would be wrong to regulate certain deadlines related to diagnosis. The regulatory framework will then become more complicated and bureaucratic for both healthcare professionals and patients, without necessarily leading to better patient outcomes. The government is concerned that all patients should be sure that they get the help they need when they need it. I believe that these legislative changes will contribute to this.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e1108	The case concerns a supplement in the Health Register Act § 8 concerning the processing of data in the Cancer Register. The Government has proposed that the current requirements of the Cancer Register Regulation for consent should be replaced by a legal right to reserve itself against the storage of data. It is also proposed to give effect to the change for information already collected. The aim of the legislative amendments is to prevent the loss of data collected and to avoid the loss of the available research capability of the Register of Cancer, to provide advice and guidance on health care for cancer diseases and to provide information to the administration and the population on measures to prevent cancer. Out of 33 hearing bodies, one has opposed such a proposal and all parties in the Health and Welfare Committee, except the Christian People's Party, support the proposal. I will explain the majority's view and expect that the Christian People's Party may explain its own. As we see it, the matter raises two main questions. The first: How can you do that? respect of privacy? Second, since the amendment to the law applies to data already collected, does it have retroactive effect? Privacy is a fundamental right and privacy is challenged in a number of areas of society. On 28 May, the majority dealt with the privacy notice, such as: points out that new technologies allow the storage and use of personal data to the extent that it was difficult to imagine in the past. In this case and in many, many other cases, the interests of individual privacy and other needs need to be balanced. These are not simple assessments. The Working Party and the majority consider that the proposed right of reservation is a reasonable balance of privacy considerations to the need for data in the Register of Cancer to be used for the research and treatment of cancer. The Data Protection Authority and the Data Protection Authority have decided to delete data on healthy persons in the Kreftregister. In order not to lose these data, the government has proposed that the change should also be applied to information already collected. The matter thus raises the second main question: about retroactive force. Does the proposal imply a retroactive effect of new legislation contrary to the Constitution? The majority have taken note of the assessment by the Ministry of Justice's Legal Affairs department, which reads as follows: «In our opinion, there can be no doubt that the proposed regulation is not affected by the Constitution § 97.» The majority share this assessment and place the decisive emphasis on the right of individuals to request information about themselves deleted. We live in a time and in a society where we must constantly be on guard against changes that weaken the individual's right to privacy. Privacy must weigh heavily, but other considerations are also legitimate. The political task is to balance considerations and seek to find solutions that can at the same time respond to privacy and the other needs of society. We believe the right of reservation solution is a good, balanced solution. We avoid a serious setback for Norwegian cancer research and at the same time grant individual ownership of their personal data.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e1137	Privacy is a fundamental right challenged in a number of areas. Article 8 of the European Convention on Human Rights provides that each person has the right to respect for his or her privacy. The intervention in the private life of individuals, including the right to have information about themselves, is contrary to the Convention unless intervention is necessary in a democratic society. Health protection is such an area. But yet this tells me that what we're discussing now is of a very fundamental nature. The requirements of the Register of Cancer provide rules for the collection and processing of health data. Section 1-2, third paragraph, which regulates data in the screening programme, states: ‘The force register may also contain health information on persons who have participated in an early diagnosis and control programme for cancer. In the case of negative findings, direct personal identification data may only be recorded permanently after consent. Direct person-identifying information may be stored temporarily in order to ensure the quality of the information.’;» Unfortunately, the Cancer Register has not acted in line with this regulation. This has been demonstrated by the Data Protection Authority through its 2009 decisions and beyond. The consent requirement is not met, consent to the voluntary, explicit and informed declaration by the data subject that he or she accepts the processing of health information about himself or herself. Let there be no doubt that the two screening programs we are dealing with today are two important screening programs. We believe that it is possible to continue to maintain the principle of consent. Health Fonna writes in her hearing that they: ‘is of the opinion that, in the interests of privacy and respect for the individual, informed consent should continue to be required. With thorough information and transparency, we believe that the needs of society will be cared for, and the person concerned will be able to maintain control of his or her own life and integrity in a better way than through right of reservation. [[and researchers]] Inform consent requires greater effort and accountability from health professionals, but with good practice the difference will not be large and it must be possible to achieve the same data base as with the use of reservation rights.» The starting point in Norwegian law is that the processing of personal data is based on voluntary consent. The processing of health data which is not based on the consent of the data subject is considered to be an intervention in privacy and requires a specific legal basis. That is why we are discussing this today. We are now going from a case where registration is accepted that those who are in the register and in the screening programs of the Kreftregister must oppose registration. The Christian People's Party believes that this is such a major interference in privacy that we cannot support the government in the changes proposed, and we therefore oppose the changes in Prop. 160 L.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e1158	In this case, it is natural to give homage to the World-Class Epidemiological Research Cancer Register. Norway may have the best cancer register in the world, based on data quality and long observation time. A little bit simplified, but right, the explanation is fractional. This research is going to be good when both the numerator and the denominator are right. The researchers around the world find their real counters – they know how many cancer patients got a diagnosis and how many survived. But they have difficulty comparing their own results over time with others or with themselves, because it's easy to find the numerator, but difficult to be sure of the denominator. In many countries, the exact size of the population is not a safe figure. For example, many countries have population census only every 10 years and then with large sources of error. Our cancer researchers have the world's best data on cancer incidence and survival because we have a quality register, and because researchers can work with safe denominators from population and personal numbering systems. I support the proposals on the subject and am pleased that political support is very broad. This is important policy backing for high-quality research for new generations. We gain new knowledge from international research, and Norway should contribute to the international community in areas where our competences and our assumptions are completely unique.

What we have been able to record is that the municipalities of the country have very different assumptions about how they can solve the problems in this important welfare area. At the same time, we see more and more tasks being assigned to municipalities, and the quality requirements become more stringent, but the instruments, and especially the financial instruments, are in many cases not proportionate to the need. The indebtedness of municipalities has also increased dramatically in recent years, and the space for action has been reduced. For a number of years, the Progress Party has called for a state funding of the care of the elderly. In Norway, it is the state that sits on the money bag, while the municipalities meet the need in the first line. The opponents of our proposal often show that it is the municipalities that are most suitable to assess the need as they are closest to the users and patients. Of course, that's quite right, but there won't be any more money. I could have understood whether those who do not support our proposal had been in favour of free municipal taxpayers, and at the same time lowered the national taxes and charges, so that the municipalities could have provided themselves with the economy needed to provide the quality of services that big-scale politicians are talking about, but no one is suggesting. What we see in Norwegian municipalities is that they lay down on a standard that is called well enough. The most important thing is to balance the limit of what is required by law and regulation, and to avoid remarks and deviations from supervision of the cheapest possible money. Unfortunately, we all too often see that the offer ends up on the wrong side of this border, with the tragic and unworthy consequences it can have. For a number of years, the Progress Party called for the state to have financial responsibility for hospitals. We were expelled and ridiculed because it was a read and assumed truth that the county council was closer to the patient than the state, and therefore better suited to being hospital owners. We had the annual blackmail between state and county, which had red numbers in their hospital accounts. So suddenly there was a political breakthrough, and the hospitals were taken over by the state, so that the state was responsible for the funding. As a result, hospitals received more financial resources, and we got a fairer distribution compared to the production of specialist health services. Thus, it was possible to sort out the hospital economy, for the benefit of patients. In addition, if we had kept the facility on the relationship between basic funding and input-driven financing that was passed on to the Progress Party, I am convinced that the health cows had become much smaller. I am now waiting for us to see a similar political awakening in the financing of the care of the elderly. The municipalities and primary health services are almost the patient, and they are often the best ones to tell about the need. The point is that when the need is duly justified and established, the money should have come automatically from the state, as we see in relation to sickness benefits, unemployment benefits, maternity leave, etc. Why is the resistance to this so great? I think that's one of two reasons: either you don't have faith in the professional assessments made by the primary health staff, or you're afraid that such a plan will become too expensive. Now I don't believe that's the first option that's up to date, because we're constantly reminded by our political opponents that the employees out there are doing a fantastic job, and we can end up doing a lot of good work in the care sector. Then it must mean that it is the second option that is the great fear: it becomes too expensive. It will be too expensive to provide old and sick elderly care and care of the quality and the extent to which big-scale politicians and government members talk about in election struggles after election struggles, and it will be too expensive to offer the dignity guaranteed by the Prime Minister in his New Year's speech. Norway is a poor country that sees need to rationalise healthcare in such a way and through such organisation and discharge. Norway is a poor country that can allow such unworthiness as will result from this system. Norway is poor who cannot see the individual, and meets it in a holistic way, according to the individual’s needs. Norway is a poor country that cannot provide the necessary safety nets in which the need governs the supply of our old and sick people who have built this society. The Progress Party will therefore continue its fight for a better service provision for our old, sick and disabled people, and we are also promoting a number of improvements here today. Understandably, we are proposing that the financial responsibility for the care of the elderly should be transferred to the state. We are promoting proposals to introduce a directive so that municipalities carry out preventive home visits to people over 75 years of age who want such an offer. This is something they have had great success with in Denmark. It seems preventive. It provides greater security for the user and allows early action, if necessary. We know that there is a lot of good care by the family and the closest to the caring one. Such a care pay scheme is practised in many places today, it becomes almost a joke. We therefore propose the introduction of binding norm rates and criteria for care pay, so that this scheme can function as intended. We believe that the elderly are important measures to give many people the opportunity to stay at home longer and yet have a social and stimulating arena to participate in. Therefore, we want earmarked operating grants for such elderly centres. In Denmark they have also had success with the project «Longest possible in their own lives», which was started in the municipality of Fredericia. This is one of the few projects in the primary health service that have been carried out with follow-up research and has proved to be a success at all levels. The users are satisfied. The employees are satisfied. And the municipality saves money. This has been proposed several times by the Progress Party, and we have been dismayed, for then in the next round to experience that the Minister of Municipal Affairs comes almost freshly home from Fredericia, where she had seen the light. Many Norwegian municipalities have taken an interest in this approach, where home helpers move on to home cleaners. But there are significant issues that you should be aware of, otherwise it may end up with failure. That's what research has shown us. The cost of the conversion itself will be slightly higher, but in the long term it may lead to a substantial reduction in costs. We believe that the municipalities here need special means of conversion, so that they can change their offer and focus from making users more intrusive to becoming more self-held. We want free user choice, and we want legal rights to family training. We see that there is a major problem with misnutrition and malnutrition in the care of the elderly, and refer to Sweden, which has taken an active step by employing people with clinical nutrition skills in all municipalities. Such a measure will prevent much unnecessary suffering and is socially economical. We want to have a review of the physical therapy reimbursement schemes to get a uniform regulatory framework for the whole country. Many today do not receive the physical therapy they need. We want a national rehabilitation and rehabilitation plan based on the model of the mental health escalation plan. This is a neglected area. We believe it is unreasonable for users in need of resource-intensive services to reduce their offer because the state grant lapses when they reach the age of 67. We want a BPA scheme where the user's assistance needs are essential for when and where and for which tasks the assistance is to be provided. We want a statutory right to hospitalization if we have a documented need. We want a progress plan to meet the need for happy day care. In our opinion, the nursing homes have not become as they were supposed to be. Nursing homes should be an alternative to a nursing home. They were to have the standard of living, and the manning was to be increased as the need for care increased. Unfortunately, we see today that nursing homes have become a lower stage of care, and that old sick and often demented people are moved to nursing homes when they have only months left to live. This is unworthiness set in the system. We need the clarification of such a plan and ensure that users and patients are offered at the right level. We will help to ensure that everyone gets exclusive access to hospitals, as promised by nine health ministers, but not yet fulfilled. We believe that patients who have to share space against their will in hospital, only have to pay 50 pst. of the consideration. This will prevent the denseness to which we see a tendency today. We want state staffing standards for nursing homes both for doctors and nurses. The increasing tasks of primary health services with patients and users with complex disorders require professional competence to be ensured. I know that many fears that manning standards can lead to that becoming a minimum standard, and I can share in discussing it the day it becomes a problem. Today we see that we have up to 80 pst. Unskilled, and we see that when professionals are away because of sickness, leave or other absence, they are replaced by unskilled workers, if at all extra staff are deployed. This is one of the few measures that businesses can use to save a few dollars in a tight budget. The back of the medal is that the quality of the offer is considerably reduced, and it can approach the indefensible in relation to patient safety. This is what we want to change. We also believe that there must be a much stronger effort to secure places for serious sick and dying. These shall be accessible by persons of all ages, where there is the possibility of combining institutional time and home time, and where there is the possibility of having their closest friends and receiving a multidisciplinary service offer that takes into account the needs of the patient as a whole. We're missing about 600 of these places today, based on the population, and we want to make sure that these are set up and secure means of operation. There are major challenges in the care sector today, and they will not be smaller in the time to come. I think everyone is genuinely concerned that good services should be provided, but the discussion is about what means we should choose to get to it. I think the last 30 years have shown us that even though a lot is good, there are a number of things that can and must get better. Let's end with the black player game between the government levels. Give professionals the opportunity to do what they are trained to do, and allow patients and users to experience the security and predictability of our welfare systems. Let people have the opportunity to maintain their human dignity and dignity even if disease or disability has affected them. With this, I would like to address the proposals put forward by the Group of the European Progressive Democrats alone, and those to which the Group of the Party of European Socialists is advocating.

The message has three main objectives: We are to acquire knowledge, explore, mobilise and deploy society's overall care resources in new ways. We need to develop new forms of care through new technologies, new knowledge, new professional methods and changes in organisational and physical frameworks. We need to support and strengthen the research, innovation and development of municipalities in this field. We need to mobilise for the joint effort on which this country is so good. We will cooperate with volunteers and relatives in new ways. We will set up a national voluntary strategy and a programme for an active and forward-looking policy of relatives. The combination of professional activity with care for children and young people, adults and the elderly with serious illness and disability shall be facilitated. We need to improve the care pay system and develop flexible relief schemes. Several men need to enter the care professions — we make the successful Men in Health from Trondheim a national measure. We're going to further bet on training volunteer coordinators in the direction of the Worldness Centre in Bergen. We want non-profit institutions to play a stronger role as well-being service providers in municipalities. Through cooperation, municipalities and non-profit actors will be better able to develop innovative actions. We shall facilitate sheltered acquisitions for non-profit actors and for long-term operating arrangements. Elder centres are a good example of measures to prevent solitude and inaction, which the Association of Pensioners has been involved in. In the voluntary strategy, we will stimulate the development of different forms of elderly and senior centres, to mention some examples. Tomorrow’s care shall consist of active care as well as care and relief. Both parts require competence. The interaction reform has led to better cooperation between hospitals and municipal health and care services and improved local health services. People who need hospitalization will get it. Hospital is an alternative when your needs cannot be resolved by your continuing to live at home, even with organisation. Therefore, the need for a nursing home is closely linked to the other services offered by the municipality. The nursing home of the future will look different, have more impact on being a home, have links with volunteers and have better cooperation with relatives. They want to invite the community in. And they're going to have competent personnel. Nursing and skilled workers should be proud to work in one of Norway’s most important jobs. We are already warning that the scheme of grants for newly built and rehabilitated health care facilities will continue after 2015. In hospitals there are also opportunities to work differently and better, raising the quality of life of those living there. It was mentioned earlier in the debate that when Bergen Red Cross's nursing home through music and monitoring patients' history, things to get up well-being and tranquillity among the patients, bring down the drug use, get more satisfied relatives, bring down the sick leave and succeed with it without having any significant more resources than other departments, we have to ask ourselves: are we good enough to learn from the success stories? If it works there, in Bergen, it must be possible to make it available elsewhere in the country. We will bring forward these success stories, give new methods the professional status they deserve and spread them systematically. The system of life assurance of nursing homes shall become a national system. But a one-sided focus on nursing homes will be going backwards into the future. I think that when many generations of Norwegians think about caring for the elderly, they automatically think about nursing homes. Most people want to stay at home as far as possible. Home services must be developed in such a way that users continue to have the most control over their own lives. Municipalities should aim to ensure that those in need of care have one contact person for coordinated services and seek to limit as far as possible the number of different persons. This will be developed in cooperation with KS and employees' organisations. Nurses, ergot therapists and physiotherapists have jointly developed a concept for everyday rehabilitation so that prevention and training can take place in one’s home environment, so that users can master the tasks of daily life. It means past efforts, and that the user's own personal goals are centered. We want to strengthen and spread this concept to more and more municipalities. The knowledge and models are there. All municipalities should now apply day-to-day rehabilitation. As it approaches the end of life, usually good care is the most important thing. It's about relieving pain, being seen and heard, washing and savoury and getting help to meet the last days of life. The goal is that unwholesome sick and dying and their relatives will experience the final phase of life as safely and meaningful as possible. We will develop a training programme for care staff to provide basic competence in care relief. People's homes need to be organized in such a way that they remain safe at home, even when physics or memory fails – or when one approaches the end of life. We also launch in the Communication a welfare technology programme to provide people with tools that provide better mastery and better lives. People with a need for care should also be able to use technology in a way that enriches their lives. Already in the revised national budget, we have launched incentives for this programme. The objective is that welfare technology should be part of the provision of services in the care services by 2020. The successful experience of building up a public care service to the extent and at the level we have done in Norway suggests that the challenges of the future must be met with two thoughts in mind at the same time: we will continue the best of what has been built up in the last 40 years, and be open to changing the course in important areas. Public care services have been growing continuously for decades. The service provision now covers users of all age groups. It is actually the number of recipients under the age of 67 who have had the strongest growth in the last 20 years. With the demographic challenges that await us fully in 10–15 years, we need to organise efforts to support and release the resources of users themselves, their families and social networks, in the neighbourhood, organisations and communities. It will require the conversion of professional activities, with a greater focus on prevention, early intervention and everyday rehabilitation. Social networking, more use of new technologies and new working methods are becoming important. At the same time, the care of the core must be processed and the medical follow-up strengthened. Uncertainty about economic development, as we see in many countries in Europe, suggests that not everything is ‘casted into concrete’. We must be able to provide good services even if there are changes in the economy, the labour market and the professional activity of its relatives. In the coming years, growth will be highest among the younger. Demographic changes therefore argue that growth in services should take place in home services, day services, rehabilitation and prevention, together with a strengthened primary health service. Think about this: If we go back 40 years in time, there were 20 000 people in the care profession. Now it's working 130 000. At that time we still picked up water in wells in parts of Oslo. People moved from homes with outdoors to modern blocks on Manglerud. We raised this society because we have taken stock, and because we have been committed to community solutions. The director and the wife were in the same hospital and the same hospital. We did it before, and we can do it again. The individual wallet should not determine whether you are well cared for. We have to offer solutions for everyone – but not for everyone, because justice is about treating people differently based on individual considerations and different needs. Only this way can people who depend to a greater or lesser extent on the care of the community continue to be the head of their own lives, even when physics and memory crunches. We will defend and develop the welfare state together. This big message shows the possibilities for it.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e1662	Tomorrow's concern is about thinking differently and smarter. We know that we live longer, and that therefore we will most likely need care services over a longer period. It can give us a challenge with enough health professionals, so we have to think differently and smarter. With new technologies and better solutions, many can cope better in everyday life, despite sickness and disability. Welfare technology can give individuals both more freedom and the ability to participate more actively. We have recently adopted a change to the law that allows dents to be equipped with GPS when they are going on their way. It gives security to relatives, but not least it gives freedom to the individual user, the freedom to go wherever you want, whenever you want – and alone, if you want. Technology can be so often. A few years ago, the committee was in Japan. There we saw the electronic harness and what it meant for demente. Here in Norway, selenium has had an undeserved reputation. We got to see how they used the harness in conversations with the demented – patients who had completely lost their language, but who, when they were in contact with the harness with the staff, started talking again. It was very nice to see how they lighted up and became engaged. There are many good technological solutions that are not yet in use. That is why I am pleased that we have municipalities that are innovative and willing to think new things, such as: Fredrikstad and Sarpsborg, participants in the EU eSenior project. They have involved the users, had conversations and identified what the need is for the individual. The project aims to pilot up to 250 homes with welfare technology for seniors so that they can live longer at home with good safety and quality of life. They're doing well and they've already put a lot of new technology into use. A lot of people are worried about technology taking over the hot hands. Of course it won't. But I must say that I would have preferred to have a sensor in my bed that could tell if it got wet there, rather than that someone would come to me in the middle of the night to stick his hand under the dune to know if it was wet. It would give a double win – not only did the caretaker pull out to check out, but I also didn't get woken up in the middle of the night. Innovation and thinking smart necessarily don't have to mean that you're going to turn everything upside down. Sometimes there are very small, simple solutions that give a countdown – like those who used vests on the caregivers who distributed medications to patients, so that others would not interfere with them while distributing medications. They were able to significantly reduce mismedication – a simple grip that brought great benefits. There are examples showing that there is a lot to learn from care innovations. Therefore, it is good that the government now implements technology programmes that will work on the establishment of open standards for welfare technology, development and testing in municipalities – and disseminate knowledge and experience on welfare technology solutions and develop good models for the introduction and use of welfare technology. Technology is important, but equally important are assistants that help people gain freedom to control their own lives. That is why I am pleased that the government has sent to the hearing a rights-holding of BPA for users with high needs. Some organisations have expressed concerns that the scheme will be weakened. That doesn't lead to correctness. The scheme is based on the obligation of the municipality today. There is nothing in the rights fixing that weakens the BPA system, but you do not solve all the challenges surrounding the organisation of the BPA. These are the challenges we're very concerned about, and we want to work on further. The important thing now is to get the rights fixation in place.

There are obvious ethical issues related to welfare technology and it is therefore important to use time to assess the boundaries between safety and monitoring. The protection of privacy must be properly safeguarded. Cooperation with users, relatives, employees and authorities on ethical issues must be central. A central question is which values could be threatened by the use of care technologies and which values could be threatened by the non-use of care technologies. This issue, I believe, shows the need for ethical reflection to be crucial for our ability to act in the best interests of the user. Care technology shall not replace human contact and care. It does not take care of the meeting between people, and care becomes unfaced and can never be a substitute for the relationship we are going to have with the weakest among us. It can create loneliness and alienation, but it can also dampen the experience of the same thing. Technology can be seen as liberating, promoting mastering and independence, but also being offensive and controlling. There is a need to strengthen the ethical competence of professionals and politicians, so that ethically challenging situations are handled in a well-reflected and good way. Care technologies in combination with human contact and care can be positive, but the Right clearly distances itself from a care technology that is used to enhance profitability and efficiency at the expense of human dignity and contact.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e1881	Good, functional and modern health buildings are important for providing patients with good health and providing good employment to employees. Population growth, the ageing population and the development of medical technology make new demands. Where do we need new hospitals, how big are they going to be, and what are they going to contain? The hospitals we already have need to be maintained and changed in line with needs. New and changed needs require good planning. The right has proposed the development of a national health improvement plan to ensure the maintenance and faster renewal of hospital property. As part of the plan, there should be an assessment of the needs for maintenance, upgrade and initial investment for the properties of specialist health services. Multiconsult estimated in 2007 that there is a need for improvement in hospitals of 55 billion. kr. In 2011, the National Audit Service addressed a maintenance need of 19 billion. kr. Local, regional and national plans for construction and property management in the specialist health service are largely lacking. According to the National Audit Office, only five out of 23 health companies have decided both the objectives and the long-term plan for the building stock. Weaknesses are also noted in the choice of the concept for hospital buildings and in the calculation of the efficiency gain associated with new buildings. Furthermore, the Right proposes that the plan contain a long-term plan for financing maintenance, upgrade and initial investment. In its consultation, the Danish medical association highlighted experiences from Denmark. In the years from 2008 to 2011, there has been an investment package divided into 16 different hospital projects. Three of them were new university hospitals. The other projects concerned modernisation, conversion and expansion. The aim of the investment was to increase the quality and productivity of hospitals. The medical association believes that there is a need for a similar investment in Norway, and they have estimated the need to be 70 billion. kr over a 10-year period of 10 billion. of which they are going to investment in medical-technical equipment. The right also proposes that the health building plan should include an adjustment to the current funding legislation whereby 70 pst. the costs of major initial investments may be financed by loans. It is gratifying to note that this is exactly what the government will achieve. It has been six months since the opposition proposed it for the first time, but the government has promoted this as a matter last week. Today, according to the Health Enterprises Act, regional health enterprises have an overall responsibility for operation and investment. It is proposed to establish a separate health building organisation to ensure the maintenance and faster renewal of hospital buildings. Parallels are drawn here to state buildings and defence buildings as property companies in the public sector. The Medical Association supports this proposal in its consultation input and refers to the possibilities offered by the organisation for permanent competence and experience learning in the management of hospital buildings. The majority, consisting of the parties to the Government, propose that the proposal for a representative be annexed to the Minutes. The minority, consisting of the Progress Party, the Right and the Christian People's Party, supports the proposal that, in the context of a more operational national health and hospital plan, a part be developed that deals with better health buildings.
