ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e1907	I shall refer to the majority's views on the matters under consideration in this article. Initially, I agree with the proposers that over several years, there have been major challenges related to capacity, patient flow and information flows in hospitals in the Oslo region. The proposers propose an estimate for the use of available capacity in private hospitals. This is not supported by the majority, as we believe that this proposal will facilitate discrimination against private and public hospitals in favour of private hospitals, because the private commercial hospitals can therefore choose which patients they are willing to investigate and treat, while the public hospitals will be left with the extensive and complex operations. While public hospitals have to give high priority to each day within defined budget frameworks, commercial actors will be able to choose patients without prioritisation as to who needs help first. The plural portions do not want such a system. Proposalers talk about efficient use of resources and the development of sufficient capacity. I can assure you that the government parties are at least equally concerned with resource and capacity utilisation. While proposers want the daily responsibility for this to lie in the Stortingen, the majority believe that the Stortingen should draw up policies and clarify the framework and that the operational responsibility should lie with health businesses. They are the ones who are the closest to assessing how, within a given framework, hospitals can be managed both in terms of operation and expansion. This brings me to the treatment of Document 8:54 S, which deals with better health structures. Good and timely health building is something we all want, and as a joint committee believes is a prerequisite for providing the best health care. Today’s government has been preparing the ground for this over the last few years by the fact that the economy of hospitals is now in order. After major financial losses that we inherited from the previous government, the hospitals are now stronger than ever. They deliver good results, treat more people and more diseases. In addition, they can now also invest in the future. Balancing the hospital economy has been a heavy but necessary process. This has also created a lot of turbulence in health regions. Everything has not been quiet while this work has been going on. No, on the contrary – modernisation work and new construction have been in parallel. Until now, this work has been carried out under public authority — of course by the fact that the construction projects have been tendered under international law, so that there has been competition in the private market for all these tasks. I record that the proposers, not unexpectedly, with enthusiasm point to Sweden, Denmark and the United Kingdom where, until now, OPS has been regarded as the good and forward-looking model for the development of both transport and other public infrastructure. When the government parties in this case draw up hospital building at the New Karolinska Solna hospital as an example of the suitability of OPS in this sector, it is because the reality description of the proposers is in stark contrast to the description in Swedish media and among Swedish health professionals. These refer to the OPS project as a scandal, which is supported by a report prepared by the Swedish conservative think tanker Timbro. This report concludes that New Karolinska has ended up at a much higher cost than the alternatives. In England it is now recognised by both finance ministers and prominent economists that OPS has been devastating to public health. Animals OPS contracts have imposed on hospitals such a large liability that the health offer now needs to be cut in order to meet the obligations towards private investors. In England, they have 20 years of experience in this type of contract and now see the full and complete consequence of the experiment. From the government parties we will warn against OPS contracts in public development projects with the simple justification: it becomes too expensive for society, for patients and for those who come after us.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e2058	I would like to comment on the proposal from the Right on health construction. Two key elements of the proposal are the possibility of a higher loan rate and the desire for OPS health building projects. As you know, the government will increase the lending framework so that health care companies can borrow up to 70 pst. of construction costs. With a healthy economy, we can now safely increase the loan share. For many health businesses this will be useful and the change is well received in the sector. At the same time, I want to mention the elementary, that as high a loan as possible has no intrinsic value in itself. A successful furniture founder from Sunnmøre once received the following question from the press: What does your company think is the most profitable, loan or lease? The sunmer replied with a smile about his mouth that he thought it was most profitable to pay in cash. Furthermore, the proposal repeats that public private cooperation in a political sense is a good solution. The question is, for which actor is that a good solution? There is already cooperation between public and private construction companies. Our hospitals are built by dividing up the large projects into tenders that private contractors win. Some are best at excavating, some at casting, some at construction, and the equipment is supplied by various specialized companies. The special feature of a political OPS model is that in addition, it shall bind itself to one operating monopolist several decades in advance. This increases bureaucracy, removes flexibility and self-righteousness and becomes costly. Knowledge of the operation of a hospital is in the organisation. An operating company with experience and knowledge of hotels, office buildings and aerodromes will operate worse and more expensive. In addition, health funds must be used to pay profits to the holding. Progress in medical technology is a pleasure to us as patients, but at the same time it is difficult to plan areas. Where today we need a lot of space because the equipment is large or needs shielding, we may get new and minimalistic equipment in a few years. New treatment can reduce the need for accommodation and beds in a subject, but increase the need for outpatient rooms. Hospital buildings are constantly changing as these changes take place. Experience suggests that a monopolist for the operation of construction becomes expensive when changes that could not be foreseen in the first tender are to be paid. Therefore, we should avoid solutions that expose and exaggerate reality.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e2259	Medical care is a central and important service for the population of Norway and is a municipal responsibility. Then it is worth remembering that this government has been committed to enabling the municipalities to provide these services also through the local economy initiative. Now it's not an easy task to do medical work in Norway. We have a long-distance country, local needs vary. Therefore, it is fundamental that municipalities should have the freedom to organise the service in line with local needs. At the same time, they must ensure good and equivalent services for all citizens. We must balance the need for government governance with the need for local freedom of action. Government governance of the local government sector shall be limited to what is necessary. Municipalities shall be provided with space for action within nationally defined frameworks. We therefore need to find a balance in governance that stimulates innovation and innovation and to establish appropriate local solutions. The government has been clear as to the direction in which we want the municipal health and care services to develop. The direction of the interaction reform is that more people receive treatment and follow-up more closely where they live. We have made clear the responsibilities of the municipalities for the general medical services and medical services through changes in the law and regulations. Work on the introduction of the national core file is an important follow-up point in the ICT message that the Stortinget dealt with in ours. Core file could make a difference in emergency medical watch situations. We have planned a limited pilot in Trondheim in the autumn of 2013 which includes medical care, emergency reception and fast doctors. In order to improve the availability of the medical watch, a single national medical watch number shall be introduced. Tests show good results. The national roll-out is scheduled for spring 20l4. As you know, work is under way on revising the emergency medical code. An important assessment is how we can best follow up our decision to impose minimum competence requirements on doctors who are to take part in medical services. Work is planned for consultation during the summer. I have noticed that the opposition is concerned that many tour operators are participating in the medical service. I have faith in many of our skilled and competent tour operators. They do important work in municipalities and hospitals, and of course they have to take part in medical care as part of training for future work in the municipal health and care service. The point is that they must have the support and follow-up of experienced colleagues. The change in basic training for doctors gives a clear indication of the important role played by the tour operators. Turnus is no longer a necessary requirement for being authorized as a doctor, but is rather a first step towards becoming a specialist. But let me stress: Turning doctors and other doctors with little experience in medical care must have other and more experienced doctors to rely on to ensure patient safety and to provide the doctor with the necessary security in the service. It is the responsibility of the municipality to provide adequate guidance during training to the tour applicants. Representatives propose to present a detailed national medical action plan in line with the proposal of the National Centre of Competence in Medical Medicine. I would like to say, as the representative Toppe, that there are many good proposals here. But it is also our position that these are proposals that are being followed up on an ongoing basis and that there is no need for an action plan. It will not solve the challenges of medical care. This is partly a question of economy, competence, capacity and equipment, and all areas deserve attention. I will continue the important work of further developing the municipal general care service and of facilitating a sustainable and competent community medical care service that ensures the proximity of the population to immediate assistance services. And so here, as the representative Toppe was about, the recruitment of the general school profession is an important part of this. There is good recruitment for the medical profession in Norway, more doctors are trained in relation to population growth, so the total number of people per doctor is going in the right direction. But the recruitment of general medicine is a challenge. We note that on the medical watch side, and that only underlines the need to have good recruitment arrangements for general medicine, and that it is being facilitated for an increasing number of women to be in this group.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e2361	A short time ago dealt with Stortinget Meld. Art. No. 15 for 2012–2013 on the prevention and combating of violence in close relations. It's an important thing. It's about violence committed by those who were supposed to be closest to us. It's about violence committed within the home's four walls, where we were really going to experience the greatest security. Especially bad is it when violence and abuse hit the most vulnerable, namely the children. The government has therefore announced its own strategy on violence and sexual abuse against children and young people. The issue we are dealing with today is one such measure to protect children from sexual abuse. It concerns changes to the Foreign Affairs Act. In this case, it is proposed to provide a basis for refusing to apply for a residence permit for special coal children in family immigration cases where the reference person, i.e. the spouse of the child's parent, in the last 10 years, has been convicted of sexual crimes against a child under 18 years of age. A Joint Committee supports the introduction of such a quarantine. It is also proposed that a residence permit may be refused if there is reason to fear that the special coal child will be subjected to sexual assault. A likelihood balance is then no longer required in such cases and a judgment is not required. An overall committee also agrees with this proposal. In cases where the reference person is intended or suspected of such a situation, it is proposed that the matter be put to rest until it is established whether this is a situation relevant to the application. A Joint Committee also supports this. This will probably not apply to very many children, but there are nevertheless important measures to protect the children concerned from sexual abuse in Norway.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e2397	This almost turned out to be the wrong posts, because the order of the cases had changed in relation to what we had on the paper from before – but I got involved in it at the last moment. So now we're dealing with changes to the Foreign Affairs Act in terms of security matters. This case concerns new rules on how management and courts are to deal with foreign matters affecting fundamental national interests or foreign policy considerations. The proposals promoted are mainly of a procedural nature. However, the Committee found that it would like to invite a hearing on the matter, since this is precisely about fundamental national interests on the one hand, and the legal certainty of individuals on the other. But no one came to the hearing. The most relevant hearing bodies are that we have expressed our opinion on the government consultation document. The opinions have been dealt with in detail in the proposition. During the Committee's proceedings, we received a letter from the Council of State setting out the need for some technical corrections to be made to the bill. It has been followed up by a unanimous proposal for adjustments in Sections 108 and 128 and a proposal for a new Section 128a. In the latter case, the right to provide for the exchange of information between public bodies on matters of fundamental national or foreign policy interest is separated by a separate provision. The Committee unanimously endorses the proposals made by the Government in this matter. This means that, hereafter, matters relating to the intervention in a foreigner’s residence permit, i.e.: expulsion, revocation or refusal of renewal, is dealt with by the Ministry of Justice and Defence as a first instance and could subsequently be brought before the courts, free of charge to the foreigner. This will speed up the process in such cases, which the Committee sees as an advantage. An additional safety-declared lawyer system is also in place, enabling more classified information to be provided. It will be an advantage for both parties. Proposals are also made which will lead to a faster treatment in security matters where the UDI remains the first instance, i.e. in cases where the non-resident has not previously resided. The proposal for a decision on the matter is therefore promoted by a unanimous committee. The only place in which the Committee is not in agreement is in the comments on the right to refugee status and protection against expulsion, including in matters of fundamental national interest or foreign policy considerations. The majority of the Committee, with the exception of the Party of Advances, is acting in this way under the Convention on Refugees as regards the right to protection and under both the European Convention on Human Rights and the Convention on Refugees as regards protection against expulsion. The Party of Progress does not share the views of the majority on the interpretation of the Refugee Convention. However, there is no impact on the situation in the case. As I said, it is unanimous. The different understanding of international commitments gives us plenty of opportunity to come back to the next case.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e2416	I note that the entire Committee supports the proposal for new rules for the handling of security matters under the Foreign Affairs Act. I would also like to thank the Committee for its excellent cooperation, which ensures that we now have a proper legal text. It is important that the authorities are able to take decisions that safeguard fundamental national interests and foreign policy considerations. To this end, the procedural rules must take account of both legal certainty and the necessary confidentiality. I believe that the proposed regulatory changes represent a good balance between these considerations. At the same time, an effective procedure is provided for. I would like to stress that these are few, but important and demanding issues. The need for confidentiality differs. The State will endeavour to ensure that decisions are based, as far as possible, on information which must not be concealed from that decision. I noticed in the position that the minority has disagreed with a majority opinion. The majority say that a non-resident cannot be denied status as a refugee for fundamental national or foreign policy reasons. On the other hand, the representatives of the Progress Party seem to believe that the Refugee Convention opens up to this. I would like to stress, as the previous speaker – the rapporteur – also pointed out, that this issue does not affect the proposed legislative changes. However, I consider it necessary to note that the opinion of the majority of the committee is correct. The right to refuse recognition as a refugee is governed by Section 31 of the Foreign Affairs Act and Article 1F of the Refugee Convention. Neither fundamental national interests nor foreign policy considerations form the basis for refusing recognition. The provisions of the Refugee Convention which the minority emphasises in support of its view do not allow for this either. They just apply to other questions. Article 9 applies to measures aimed at individuals in war situations or similar situations. Article 2 establishes that refugees are obliged to comply with the laws of the country of destination. Article 33 provides for the expulsion of a refugee for reasons of national security but not for the purpose of refusing recognition as a refugee. I would like to stress that Norwegian law also opens up the expulsion of refugees for such reasons. However, a return may never take place as long as there is a real risk of serious infringement.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e2454	In this case, the majority shall consider a proposal from the Progress Party for major changes to the Foreign Affairs Act. The starting point is that Norwegian immigration policy is determined by the Norwegian authorities, but a minimum requirement is that we fulfil our international obligations under general international law and the international agreements we are bound by. Today’s Foreign Affairs Act was adopted by the Stortinget in April 2008 and entered into force on 1 January 2010, following extensive work of broad political consensus. The Foreign Affairs Act and its associated regulations are in themselves a complex regulatory framework for the regulation and control of immigration and residence of foreigners in the Kingdom. The proposals of the Progress Party are comprehensive and were allocated to the Committee just three weeks ago, on Monday 27 May. However, the Committee has chosen to deal with the matter before the Grand Duchy separates. Despite the wide range of legislative proposals, it is relatively easy to decide. It is largely a reprisal of Storting’s treatment of new foreign law in 2008. That is also the reason why the Committee chose not to hold a hearing in the case. Attached to it are letters from the Minister for Justice and Security, reviewing the main proposals for changes. Just as the former Minister of Labour and Inclusion assessed it in 2008, the current Foreign Minister concludes that a number of proposals violate Norway’s basic international foreign commitments. For all the other parties, this is enough reason in itself to argue that the proposals are not acceptable. As in 2008, the proposals will only have the own votes of the Progress Party. In the context of a 5-minute post, it is not possible to comment on all the proposals, but their direction is already estimated in the context of the purpose paragraph. The Progress Party proposes here to introduce a restrictive interpretation of general international law and international conventions. There are already international driving rules for this. We cannot make our own practice contrary to that. In addition, the use of terms such as the fact that the law applies only to those who are actually persecuted expresses strong scepticism that many of those who are given refugee status today are not actually persecuted. There's an opinion The Progress Party is all about. Several of the proposals run counter to international commitments. The proposers believe that Norway should interpret the Schengen Agreement in its own way by lowering the requirement of border control. We don't have the power to do that. It will be more difficult to stay in Norway for a number of groups, even for persons entitled to protection. The Progress Party will send them back to the region they came from, and let other countries take care of them. Immigrants shall no longer be allowed permanent residence. Nor should they be entitled to take their families to Norway. There is no breach of international conventions, but nevertheless not very smart, for the purpose of recruiting future workers. However, what is a violation of international conventions are the proposals to lower the threshold in order to deprive people with a need for protection of their refugee status, cf. the previous case, proposals to restrict the possibility of staying due to strong human considerations, to repeal the requirement of the Convention on the Rights of the Child as a fundamental consideration in matters relating to children, and to introduce a new immigration regulatory recital, called integration considerations, but the latter is, in substance, a discrimination against people. Some, based on ethnic dividing lines, must meet more stringent requirements than others with regard to the possibility of permanent residence and with regard to the possibility of family reunification with both spouses and children. Proposals are also made for a reduced threshold for the use of coercive substances, including: closed receipts for those who are unable to document their ID on arrival. The sum of all the proposals being promoted violates the Racial Discrimination Convention, the European Convention on Human Rights, the United Nations Convention on Social and Political Rights and the Children's Convention. Less serious, but just as no other party wants to join, the stricter rules for both permanent and temporary stays will lead to considerable administrative extra work, contrary to what the proposers themselves say they want. As I said, the proposals are not supported by any other parties. I assume that the proposers themselves address the proposals they wish to make in the matter.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e2482	When this proposal was drawn up, we asked the Ministry of Justice for a legislative review of the proposals. This was rejected. We asked again for a legal review, based on the letter of reply from the department. We got a new rejection. The reason we did that was to avoid getting into a situation where some claims were made that this proposal violates international conventions. Now the Council of State does not say that it is in breach of international conventions, as the President of the case said, but the Council of State says that they can be broken. And that's a difference for me. Finland has made some changes to its legislation in order to reduce immigration, and they have streamlined the deployment of illegal immigrants. Denmark has done the same. In England, new laws are currently being discussed to limit immigration, and in Switzerland, the same thing is being done. As far as I know, they do not break any international conventions. In their letters, the Council of State writes that we can break some conventions, and that there is a great difference between the proposal we have made and what they have presented in Denmark. Just before I went to the hall, I went through the rules on immigration to Denmark, and I actually couldn't see the huge differences. In the Progress Party, we are reasonably sure that we are staying within the conventions that are, and if it turns out that when we introduce this, we would be able to break with someone, of course, to come forward with proposals that make us stay within. But there is clearly a difference between the interpretation of international laws. Norway has greater immigration than both Denmark and Finland combined. Then it's allowed to ask why it's like this. There must be something about the law that makes coming to Norway more attractive than coming to these countries. If we want to reduce immigration, we can do that. But if you're satisfied with the immigration flow we have to Norway, of course you leave it. But the reason why the Progress Party is promoting the proposal – and the intentions of this proposal – is that we want sustainable immigration and immigration that will not go beyond the well-being we have in Norway today. I've said it before, and I'll say it again: everything is possible – it just depends on how you do it, and if you want to do it. Some time ago, they were signed in Finland and looked at immigration and integration policies there. We visited, for example: Helsingsfors administrative court. One of the proposals we have made here is to lay down UNE and get an administrative court in Norway. We had a thorough review of the Administrative Court and how the Administrative Court worked in Finland. The Helsinki Administrative Court deals with approx. 8 000 cases per year, and approximately 20 pst. – or about 2000 of them – they said, referred to asylum or immigration issues. It was said that almost 100 pst. complaints against decisions in asylum cases, but that the administrative court maintains decisions in any case in 70 p.m. of these matters. In cases where cases are sent back to Migri, which is found to respond to UDI, for new treatment, it is due to changing assumptions in the home country. And that is important: the changed assumptions that can be made in the home country must be taken into account by an administrative court, and that takes into account the Finnish Administrative Court, so that it can be used when the final decision is taken. What was the main issue with the Administrative Court was respect for decisions. It was throughout the entire administration and for the politicians we talked to in Finland. There was a much greater respect for decisions taken in an administrative court than there was for an administrative decision taken by the administration. So it must also be possible for Norway to assess whether an administrative court is right to have here, so that we get a safer, safer, and more efficient treatment of asylum cases. With this – since I see that the time has come – I am promoting the proposals made by the Progress Party in the matter.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e2511	The centre party will pursue an immigration policy that puts human dignity in the focus and where individual people have clear rights and obligations. The Central Party will have a consistent asylum policy ensuring legal certainty and human rights. Employment migration to Norway represents a significant contribution to the population surplus in all regions. It is an important factor in the Norwegian economy, at the same time confronting us with new challenges as to how the welfare state should be structured. The proposals we are considering today in the United Kingdom clearly show that, with regard to some fundamental aspects of immigration and integration policy, there is a clear distinction between the Progress Party on the one hand and the other parties on the other. It can be done, among other things: on the understanding of reality and the relationship with Norway’s international obligations. Immigration policy is constantly being challenged – and not just by the Progress Party. On the one hand, we have those who claim that policy is naive and unsuccessful, that immigrants have too easy access to Norway. On the other hand, we have those who believe that Norway is overly strict, even inhuman and hearty, policies towards immigrants. Sometimes it is difficult to understand that it is the same immigration policy that both criticise. Personally, I welcome the debate. I think it's healthy, and I would have been far more concerned if we hadn't had any debate, or if the criticism had only come from one edge. Immigration policies are largely concerned with balancing different considerations. It's demanding, it's necessary – and we know that we can't make everyone ready. Although immigration policy is largely about balancing different considerations, there are some considerations that alone weigh so heavily that in my view they should not be subject to assessment. As a responsible member of the world community, we cannot assess, in the view of the Centre Party, whether Norway should comply with its international obligations. Persecuted persons seeking asylum in Norway are entitled to protection and to humane and equitable treatment, whether they like it or not. Many of the proposals made by the proposers have been made in the past. The majority of the Committee, all with the exception of the Progress Party, consider that several of the proposals are either contrary to – or must be presumed to be contrary to – the international commitments of Norway. We should be open to debate also on immigration policy, but not on proposals that violate our international obligations. In addition, several of the proposals are costly and may prevent integration, which is the very opposite of what we really need. Stricter rules for immigrants at work, including: with reduced family reunification, e.g. both reduce the quality of life of individuals and probably also prevent the recruitment of the specialist skills needed by Norway. The proposers have set out six so-called guidelines, which they believe must be the foundation of Norwegian immigration policy. The majority of the Committee considers this to be a comprehensive description of today’s Norwegian welfare society. We have come a long way in gender equality in Norway, even when we compare with other European countries. We have a high level of employment, low unemployment and good welfare arrangements. In addition, integration has been better than most other countries. We are at the top of the world in terms of assistance, and our country is characterised by a high level of trust between the inhabitants and between the inhabitants and the authorities. Immigrants make up a little over 13 pst. of the population representing 12 pst. of the total employment. Many immigrants are already of great importance as a resource in Norwegian working life, but there is no doubt that too many people are out of work. It's a problem for those involved, and it's a problem for society. That as much as possible has a job to go to, a home to live in and are active in the community is three criteria for successful integration. This is where both society and individual immigrants have an important responsibility. The central party believes that the key to integration lies in activity — activity in the workplace, in school and in the community. It is therefore necessary to specify that immigrants are to be settled in a municipality as soon as possible, initiate an introduction programme and get out of work.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e2572	The current proposal for a representative of the Committee concerns the main principles of the whole immigration and refugee policy. The government will pursue a humane, fair and consistent asylum policy and is concerned with the balance between the various considerations to be taken into account. The government has implemented a number of restrictions during the last two periods. The number of asylum seekers has fallen since 2008-2009, and a greater proportion of those arriving have a real need for protection. Last year this resulted in that in 54 pst. protection was granted by all matters dealt with in substance. The proposed representative places disproportionate emphasis on control and immigration regulation. International obligations and humanitarian considerations are set aside, and a number of new conditions are proposed that would make the proceedings disproportionately resource-intensive and square. One of the overall elements of the proposal is the restrictive interpretation of general international law and international conventions. The proposers will delete what it says today that the law should be applied in accordance with international rules by which Norway is bound, when these are intended to strengthen the position of the individual. Following the proposal, only the EEA rules and agreements between the Nordic countries shall be referred to. The proposal would clearly show a completely different attitude to international human rights conventions than is known to Norway today. I have drawn up examples in my letter of 4 June of the fact that the proposal for a representative on a number of points contradicts – or seems to require a practice contrary to – our obligations under international law. I would add that when the committee rapporteurs focus on which asylum seekers can ‘document’ that they are at risk of persecution, I am further concerned about whether the proposers take the Refugee Convention seriously. No «documentation» may be required for persecution; potential offenders rarely give advance notice in writing. I record that the representative Morten Ørsal Johansen stated to Dagbladet on 7 June that the policy of the Progress Party is in line with that implemented in Denmark and Finland. Although it is true that many elements of the legislative proposals of the proposers are inspired by the rules of Danish legislation, it is so many and important differences that it is completely wrong to set similarities. Comparison with Finnish legislation is becoming even more misleading. It will lead far too far to go into all the details, but I feel the need to bring something forward, since also the representative Ørsal Johansen came up with this earlier today: Both Denmark and Finland comply with the conditions of the Schengen acquis relating to internal border controls. Both countries comply with the rules of the Refugee Convention on exclusion from refugee status. Neither Denmark nor Finland has such a practice as proposed by the proposers to refer refugees to seek protection in neighbouring countries. None of the countries has the right to refuse humanitarian residence for groups of nationality who experience greater integration challenges than others. Both countries have legislation and practices that allow attention to health and humanitarian issues in the evaluation of asylum cases. Both countries have provisions in the expulsion legislation that either refer to international obligations or impose their own requirements to assess the proportionality of expulsion. None of the countries have such strict prison rules as proposed in the Document 8 proposal that asylum seekers are ‘to be imprisoned’ until the identity is established. These are just some of the many important examples of differences between the proposal and the policy in Denmark and Finland. Therefore, the relationship between the proposals put forward and the rules of international law cannot be explained by reference to Danish and Finnish legislation. I also see this that the representative Ørsal Johansen has recognised in Dagbladet on 12 June. In addition, I would like to refer to the letter that I have sent to the Committee today, where I will go further into the details of these questions. It leads too far to address the details, but this should contribute to further documentation of the issues raised in the proposal.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e2910	The proposal concerns two separate cases — for the first administrative decision against host families who abuse the aupair system, so-called quarantine provisions, and for the second expulsion as a result of criminal offences committed before the granting of the initial authorisation and the implementation of decisions for EEA citizens with the right of residence under Section 111 of the Foreign Affairs Act. With regard to the aupair scheme, we are only reminded that Norway acceded to the Council of Europe's agreement on the recruitment of au pair in 1969 – an agreement on which we are of course bound. This means that au pairs shall have time to attend language courses and work a maximum of five hours a day. The number of persons granted au pair residence permits has increased, and today's au pairs have changed slightly since the 1960s. They now come from developing countries to a greater extent than was usual in the past. The proposal on quarantine schemes aims at strengthening the cultural aspect and preventing au pairs from being used as cheap labour. Under the foreign regulation, a foreigner may receive a residence permit as an au pair in Norway for a total of two years when the contract meets the more detailed requirements of the UDI. In return for the stay in the host family, au pairen performs certain tasks such as facilitating housework, care of children, etc. Au pairen must be between 18 and 30 years of age and must be able to participate in Norwegian training. Today, quarantine is not allowed against host family who violates the conditions of aupair residence. An overall committee indicates that the purpose of the scheme is to allow for international cultural and language exchanges at an interpersonal level, through the presence of young people in a Norwegian family. In return, the au pair contributes with defined benefits, as mentioned above. The Committee agrees that there are conditions under which the relationship is not, or is developing to be, an exploitation of the au pairs as cheap, unskilled labour, and we believe that the proposals will contribute to the protection of the fair use of the auair system by providing for administrative decisions allowing the host family to be quarantined if the conditions for the auair maintenance are violated. We also believe that the proposals for penalties have been made at a reasonable level as long as there is a breach of the specific conditions of the residence permit in connection with the stay. On one point, the Committee has shared its views. The parties to the Government shall form the majority of the Committee. They believe that, in principle, it would be nice to have had a common Nordic regulatory framework in this area, but they do not think it is very practical how the rules are designed in the other Nordic countries today. As you know, Finland and Sweden do not have rules on quarantine, and the Danish rules contain rules that differ slightly from those proposed in the proposal. The members of the Committee from the Progress Party, the Right and the Christian People's Party would nevertheless have considered it an advantage if we had been able to approach the Danish rules, which are only marginally different, because it had given the Nordic a greater degree of justice seen from the outside. Then we could work for Finland and Sweden to come after. Some of us have enjoyed participating in the Nordic Council. That's why we don't take more account of each other when we have new legislation in the process, constantly up. This is an example of how you can't seize the possibilities. Then the members of the Committee from the Progress Party raised an interesting issue, and it is whether or not the au pair can be held responsible for the absence of tax deductions from the host family. The Progress Party states that any sanctions must be directed against the employer, not against the employee. It's an interesting issue, but it's on the side of the matter. We're not unwilling to go into that discussion in another context. An overall committee would consider the demarcation made in the personality, which may trigger quarantine sanctions to be appropriate. Finally, the proposed second amendment is two clarifications in the Foreign Affairs Act: that criminal convictions for matters that were not known at the time of granting a permanent residence permit shall be assessed on the basis of the rules applicable to temporary residence permits. This is quite logical, as the path to a permanent residence permit is about a temporary residence permit, and the durability of it would depend on whether complete information has been provided when applying for a temporary residence permit. One should not then be able to rely on the increased connection that a permanent residence permit provides, if it has been obtained on wrong premises and detained data.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e2938	I am going to be quick, because the rapporteur has presented the matter in a clear way, and we share the most, most of the views expressed by the rapporteur. I just want to comment on a little bit about a common Nordic legislation in this area. The majority have nothing against a common Nordic legislation, but we believe that the government's proposal in this matter is better than the Danish system, precisely because the government's proposal requires administrative decisions to be taken in all cases concerning the quarantine of host families. This is not the case in Denmark, where, among other things: is given a judgment. The adoption of administrative decisions will, first of all, prevent the granting of residence as an au pair in cases where the host family actually has quarantine, which may happen if the quarantine is automatically imposed in the extension of a judgment in the judiciary. Secondly, the requirement for administrative decisions will provide better information to the host family on the content of the decision. They will also have the right to complain. Thirdly, the requirement for administrative decisions in all cases will make it easier to keep statistics and to follow up on the use of quarantine provisions. As regards the minority proposal from the Progress Party, the majority does not vote for it. I support the reasons given by the rapporteur on that point.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e2997	The aim of the aupair scheme is to allow for international cultural and language exchanges at an interpersonal level by a youth living with a Norwegian family and in addition receiving language instruction. In return, the au pair contributes with defined services such as easier housework, childcare, etc. for limited time per day. The center party believes this is a good arrangement that we have to fence around. The scheme itself is well incorporated and relatively detailed, at the latest with provisions that entered into force in 2012. It is a good system for language and cultural exchange, and most people respect the legislation so that the scheme provides a win-win situation for the host family and for the au pair. Unfortunately, in some cases this has evolved into a regular exploitation of au pair as cheap, unskilled labour. They are in an extremely vulnerable situation because they both live and live with the employer – if you can call it that. The applicable law does not allow the adoption of administrative decisions against families which violate the conditions of aupair residence. The parties therefore support proposals for amendment to Section 27 of the Foreign Affairs Act. If a host family seriously or repeatedly violates the rules to protect the au pair conditions, a decision may be taken not to grant a residence permit to new au pairs of the family concerned. If the host family infringes the conditions, the Directorate of Foreign Affairs may decide to apply quarantine for one, two or five years. If a person in the host family has been in prison for more than three months for conditions committed against an au pair in that host family, a decision on quarantine may be taken for up to 10 years. Quarantine provisions aimed at host families could be preventive and a contribution to the protection of the fair use of the aupair system. One could prevent the use of illegal labour and prevent these homes from becoming an arena for forced labour and, in the worst cases, trafficking in human beings. As far as trafficking in human beings is concerned, this is, of course, covered by the current provisions of the penal code and will have to be prosecuted in the usual way, regardless of the context in which it has occurred. The Central Party agrees that a common Nordic regulatory framework in this area could in principle be desirable. However, the current situation does not make it appropriate, as neither Sweden nor Finland have provisions on quarantine. We therefore support the government's proposal that the Foreign Affairs Directorate should be able to take decisions on quarantine in order to prevent the granting of au pairs' residence permits in a family that has committed serious violations of the guidelines for host families or where family members have committed criminal offences against former au pairs.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e3025	I am pleased that the Committee endorses the government's proposal to introduce a quarantine provision for host families who abuse the aupair system. The law on quarantine is one of several measures aimed at tightening up and strengthening the cultural aspect of, the aupair system and preventing au pairs from being exploited as cheap or illegal labour. The dependency ratio between an au pair and its host family allows au pairs to be particularly vulnerable to exploitation. The quarantine system may prevent abuse, prevent social dumping and contribute to preventing future infringements of the terms of the au pair residence permit of both the host family and other host families. And I agree with the representative, Becquerad, that we have to follow developments, and the impact of the changed frameworks of the scheme carefully. The possibility of quarantine could contribute to the protection of the rights of au pairs with regard to duties, living conditions and leisure time. In addition, the proposal could help prevent forced labour, trafficking in human beings and other criminal offences. The law establishing a quarantine system provides that a decision may be taken not to grant an au pair residence permit to a host family which has seriously or repeatedly breached au pair residence permit conditions. The quarantine may be valid for one, two or five years. Decisions may also be taken on the basis of penalties or special reactions imposed in respect of facts committed against an au pair. The quarantine may then be valid for up to 10 years. Host families that have abused the aupair system cannot thus get a new au pair during this period. When the amendment enters into force, it will not be possible to obtain an application for a residence permit to be au pair with a family subject to quarantine on grounds of abuse or punishment. This will make it safer to be au pair in Norway. The minority of the Committee has noted that it considers it beneficial to have equal rules in the Nordic region and that quarantine should automatically occur as a result of the conviction of certain criminal offences against the au pair, as in Denmark. I agree with the majority of the Committee that, in principle, common Nordic legislation is desirable in this area, but that it is not appropriate as it is today in the other Nordic countries. Neither Sweden nor Finland provides for quarantine. In Denmark, quarantine takes place automatically on the basis of a conviction, while in the case of quarantine, the alienation authority takes a decision when specific conditions are met. I think it is most appropriate to have the foreign authorities decide on the quarantine in all cases. Quarantine is an administrative response to which a decision should be attached. The host family then receives information about the decision and the possibility to complain about it if necessary. The members of the Committee of the Progressive Party have proposed that the responsibility of the employer for the payment of taxes be clarified in the legislative text and that this be followed by a response in line with what is otherwise proposed in the new provision on quarantine. This issue was raised in the consultation round. My assessment is that it is not appropriate to include the non-payment of taxes by the quarantine facility.

See if we in the Left are very busy that the library should be free of charge, and that all the basic offers should be free of charge, I think we have to bet on such things as connecting the trade library – for example. for different occupational groups — towards libraries, such as: The homeopaths in Oslo can rent a library service from a library to build a trade library and follow up on professional debates. I think that's the kind of thing we need to bet on in the future. These are the development venues we have for the libraries in the future, and it's important to set a framework for that. So we're going to vote for the proposal as it stands from the government. But I think this is a very exciting field to follow on, and I think the point is to tear down shots and give action spaces to development – because none of us fully know what a library looks like in 30-40 years.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e3105	The public libraries have a fundamentally important democratic function, and I am very pleased with the broad connection that the committee gives to the proposals for changes in the law on public libraries. The government's ambitions for the library sector were mentioned in the 2009 library message that was launched by the Stortinget. As a follow-up to the message, the government has come up with a bill to change the law on public libraries, and the goal is to facilitate robust, resilient public libraries so that all citizens in the country can get good library services. Digital developments — the increasing availability of knowledge and literature online — also mean that the role of libraries in society is changing. It is important that libraries fulfil their function both physically and digitally. The public libraries shall fulfil their democratic mandate digitally by making PCs available for free of charge and by using the librarian’s competence to systematise and filter the choice of data and information available digitally. The availability of e-books should be a natural part of the services of public libraries, and today it is a municipal responsibility. In addition, in the case of books from purchasing systems, the Culture Council has launched a pilot project in which different distribution solutions will provide an experience base on which it can be further developed in municipalities. Although books and literature are the core of a library, studies show that there are less than half of the users who borrow books from the library. This clearly shows how the role of libraries in society is changing. In many municipalities, libraries have developed to be meeting places in the community, meeting places that are experienced as both open and relevant to all. This important function suggests to the government that the law on public libraries also be reflected by the fact that the libraries as an independent meeting place and the venue for public conversation and debate are set out in the purpose paragraph. Although the current law only obliges the libraries to make books available free of charge, the public libraries have to a large extent also active dissemination. Now the government suggests that active dissemination is also highlighted in the purpose paragraph as a central task of a people's library. Professionally relevant competence is fundamental to good library services and forward-looking development. The government is proposing that further demands be made for a qualified librarian, and the regulatory work that will follow the legislative changes will require a qualified librarian to be audited. There has been a development in the type of education relevant to a librarian, and the training has changed from a given course of study to a composition of different professional combinations. In order to maintain the autonomy of the municipality, the government suggests that it is still possible to dissociate from the competence requirement if a qualified applicant does not submit. A regulation will lay down the conditions for exemption and the temporary derogation will be removed. It is important that the municipalities are committed to developing the one assigned to the position of librarian, from the outset, which would prevent the temporary exemption from being granted. The county councils are in the middle of a process of change in which municipal tasks are directed towards guidance and coordination. Today the library functions in the counties are organized very differently, and in the bill the government proposes an update of the law to reflect this. It is no longer natural for the county councils to be required to maintain the county library as their own organisation, and we propose to facilitate the development of the county's library tasks towards guidance and coordination. We still believe that it is important that the county council has library professional competence at the leadership level, and we maintain a claim to that effect. The directive on cooperation between public and school libraries will be removed. It is the individual who has responded. But the reason is that it is quite obvious that these two institutions should cooperate, but it is unnecessary to impose an organisational unit in one municipality to cooperate with another organisational unit in the same municipality. That's what I said they'd do. It is also a point that imposed the way it was designed, only applied to the public libraries and not to the school libraries. At the same time, we remove a number of can-destinations, i.e. tasks that the municipality and county councils are still free to do, and that will contribute to better library services, but that there is no requirement for them to do, and therefore it is not as natural for them to be mentioned in the law.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e3154	The size is today invited to adopt changes to the Media Ownership Act. The Committee shall submit a split opinion on the matter. The majority are in favour of implementing the proposed amendments, while a minority consisting of the Right Party and the Progressive Party do not support the proposed amendment. I assume that representatives from these two parties will explain their views on this matter. It matters who owns the media, also in Norway. The Media Ownership Act is based on the fact that large parts of the public conversation and the exchange of views take place in the mass media. Monitoring of the dissemination of news therefore offers great opportunities to influence opinion. The majority emphasise that it is also important, for democratic reasons, that this influence should not be concentrated in one or a few actors. This is what forms the basis of the Media Ownership Act. For the majority, it is essential to stress that a key prerequisite for genuine freedom of expression is the diversity of free and independent mass media. Then we should be clear that the bill, as it is presented, is not based on the documented consequences of ownership concentration in today's media market. The law is primarily an insurance policy – a precautionary law – for a media future we know little about. We do not know what kind of owners of Norwegian media will get in the future, but what we know is that the greater the concentration of ownership we open to in Norway, the greater the consequences of any purchase. The government also believes that such a perspective provides the basis for the continued need for a separate law of media ownership. The majority support such a view. We all see that there is clearly a need to adapt the law to technological and market developments in the media sector. The electronic mass media, including the network and mobile services of newspapers and TV channels, are now central channels for the dissemination of news and societal debate in Norway. A law that does not include electronic channels will therefore be based on an out-of-date media image, and a law that does not equate electronic and traditional media use will give a misleading picture of ownership positions in the sector. The regulation of media ownership is one of several instruments in this area that work together to meet the overall objectives of freedom of expression and media pluralism. Media support is also a good tool to provide a financial basis for the diversity of media businesses in Norway. We record that the minority does not see such a relationship in their budgetary observations. They want to cut or remove the press support, and they will not support this amendment. In my opinion, the minority helps to tear away the foundation of the great media diversity that we are so lucky to have in Norway – but they do. For the majority, it is important to clarify that the Media Ownership Act, together with the Media Editorial Freedom Act, facilitates this diversity of media businesses to also act as truly independent sources of information and public voices. With the support of a majority in this Chamber, this proposal proposes several amendments to the Media Ownership Act. For time's sake, I don't have time to go into the details, except to refer to the committee's recommendation. However, it is worth noting that the changes involve a shift to a more flexible regulatory model by leaving more of the more detailed regulation to regulation. In retrospect, we have recorded a debate on whether it is contrary to Section 100 of the Constitution that the government’s media supervision is proposed to have a combined role – usually both the donor and the supervisory authority. In doing so, the Committee has received an investigation from Schibsted ASA which concludes that the government's proposal is contrary to the Basic Law § 100. This investigation has been under assessment at the Legal Department of the Ministry of Justice, which concludes that the bill does not conflict with Section 100 of the Constitution. The majority also note that according to the Law Department’s assessments, the legislative proposal cannot be criticised as left-hand work – as some have suggested – and therefore supports the government’s legislative proposals.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e3310	Mass media are the most important arenas we have for wide news coverage and political debate. It was thoroughly documented in the latest power formation. It also means that control of the media gives potentially great power, a power that can become a democratic problem, if it is abused. The Media Ownership Act is therefore an important democratic security mechanism. At the same time, it is necessary to ensure that the law adapts to changes in the media landscape. The government has therefore proposed to extend the law to include rules on ownership in electronic media. Otherwise, the most important change is a conversion to a more flexible control model, by leaving the closer regulation to regulations. The idea is that this will give a more accurate regulation, and therefore that any intervention is better grounded and better cared for the purpose of the law. The law is controversial – something else may not be waiting. He puts serious obstacles in the way of the potential of the major media players to expand through acquisitions and mergers. It is obvious that the law is a tower aside for part of the players in the industry. As legislators, it is important that one is aware that the Media Ownership Act does not exist for the sake of the industry – the law should ensure media failure and freedom of expression for citizens, including through times of major structural changes for the media sector. It is also important to remember that those for whom the law is applicable do not have the same voice and resources as the major industry players have. In this context, it is natural to comment on the debate during the last weeks on the relationship with Section 100 of the Constitution. Schibsted has been produced with a report that concludes that the proposal is contrary to the Constitution, primarily because the Constitution sets strict limits on the relationship to the delegation of legislative authority in the areas of freedom of expression. With all due respect for the prominent lawyers behind the report, I think it's a derailment of the debate. The relationship with the Basic Law § 100 has been assessed on several occasions, both by the department itself and by the expert group who has taken out the basis for the government's proposal for changes in the law. However, on the basis of a request from the Committee on the subject, it seemed reasonable to ask the Ministry of Justice's Legal Affairs Department for an additional assessment of this issue. The Legal Department clearly concludes that the proposal for changes to the Media Ownership Act does not conflict with the Constitution. It's not surprising. I think that there should clear up all doubts in the matter, and that there should now be no obstacle to the adoption of the government's proposal for changes to the law. The Right Party and the Progress Party wish to leave the ownership of the media area to universal competition law, with some additions to the Competition Act as appropriate. I think this is going to be wrong, for a simple reason: mass media publishing is something far more than just business. In addition, the mass media has an important democratic role in society by being a central public arena for news communication and political societal debate. In contrast to the Competition Act, which aims to ensure efficient use of resources, the Media Ownership Act shall ensure media failure and freedom of expression. There is a wide range of considerations, and the results of these assessments may also be different. This is something that many countries in Europe have realised, and there are also very many who have rules on media ownership in one form or another. What all of these have in common is that they have concluded that competition law alone is not sufficient in the media field, whether it be the United Kingdom, France, Germany, Spain, Belgium or Austria. That's why we're not alone. So I want to comment on a couple of points in the present bill that have been particularly controversial. It applies for the first time to define its scope of action in relation to electronic media, and for the second, to the need for regulation of ownership at regional level. When it comes to electronic media, some professionals have taken the word for either – or – approximation. So either network and mobile media should not be covered by the law at all, or all such media should include, i.e. also social media and search engines, including actors such as Facebook, Google and Twitter. Either way, the result would be the same thing. One would ensure that the Media Ownership Act would not have any practical significance in this area. I think that if you have ambitions to adapt the law to today's media reality, we must at least make sure that he will apply to the most important dissemination platforms. When it comes to ownership regulation at regional level, the question arises: is it possible to ensure the necessary ownership failure with national borders alone? I think the answer is no. One example that we use in the proposition is that an actor – in theory – will be able to own all the localities in all of North Norway, Trondelag and Westland north of Stavanger without crossing the border on one third of any national market.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e3472	In the Document 8 proposal from the Progress Party on a modernised book market, the government is asked to terminate the book agreement scheme. When we have proposals for book law at the same time, the system is replaced by a book or industry agreement, but the exemption from the competition law for book business is continued. The Progress Party does not want this, while the Right and Christian People's Party wants to continue today's system with an industry agreement. I guess the minorities explain their views in their own articles. Government parties now welcome book law in Norway. This is a day of joy. It's been a long time since the work on book law started, and finally we're in port. Norwegian literature policy has been a success, where the industry agreement has been essential. Now replacing it with a mandatory law will create predictability in the industry. It will ensure a long-term work on breadth, quality, diversity and accessibility in Norwegian literature – precisely what is the main objective of literature policy – as well as on the preservation and further development of Norwegian languages, to the delight of readers and authors. The proposal for book law is based on a balance between competition considerations and cultural policy considerations. Literature is simply too important to be left to the market alone. One important element of the book law is fixed prices – i.e. obligations for suppliers to set a binding price on new books. This does not mean that the price has to be set high, as some opponents of fixed prices like to claim, but one does not have the opportunity to dump the prices of a small sample of best sellers. The literature in Norway should be a much more diverse literature than it will be if only the market regulates the situation. Today, publishers can bet on narrower literature because these publications are funded by the sale of the literature. If it's only profitable to produce books one knows will sell well, it'll be as good as impossible for the new voices, debutants and the new writing modes. If we want new writers, someone has to dare bet on the unknown. Today's best-selling writers have also been insecure short once. To put it that way, the words of Tom Egeland: «Norwegian literature policy has helped to grow them up to popular-loved authors». This was a reference to e.g. Petterson, Saabye Christensen and Wassmo. Both for readers and authors, the fixed-price system is therefore important – for the income of authors and the ability to reach readers. The proposal also lays down provisions allowing suppliers, authors and translators to cooperate on normal contracts. Without these, each writer and translator is left to himself in the negotiations with the publishers, which naturally will be difficult for new authors and writers of narrow books. Literature is not a common commodity. We want a variety of books from a variety of writers in different songs. We want a book business that can afford to take chances, bet on something new and unknown, and experimental literature. This will hardly survive on the free market. The Progress Party is largely opposed to literature policy instruments and has a fierce belief in the excellence of the market, and competition will ensure diversity also in the book industry. If we take a quick comparison between countries, we see that Norway publishes more titles and sells more books than in our neighbouring countries, and we see that a trend with decreasing numbers of bookstores is more evident in free-price countries than in bookstore countries. If we are to ensure good quality books, at the same price throughout the country, we must have good literature policy tools. The right and Christian People's Party supports the instruments, but wants to continue with a voluntary trade agreement instead of introducing a book law. In no way has it been possible for one to get into port with a possible new agreement – and since 2005, we have seen a flourishing of publishers outside the industry agreement and who are not bound by the fixed-price scheme either. The inclusion of cultural policy instruments should not be voluntary. In the form of law it is the legislator who has the control of the instruments, not the industry itself. I am pleased that the book is covered by the Book Act. It ensures a breadth and diversity in both large and small subjects, which is essential for developing Norwegian as an academic language. There have been major changes in the book industry in recent years, and developments are continuing. Therefore, an evaluation of the e-book market is provided after three years. Similarly, an evaluation of the impact of the margin regulation between suppliers and dealers will also be carried out in the light of the input we have received in consultations. I think this is a good piece of legislation we have to deal with. Through this and other literature policy instruments, such as: purchasing system, production support and grant and remuneration schemes for authors – we ensure that the population across the country has access to a wide range of quality literature from a variety of authors, themes and genres.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e3498	The red-green cultural vows had the ambition to make Norway a major European cultural nation. One of the most important grips of Jack Lang was that in 1982 he introduced a book law in France. This was to feed the French language, and ensure a great diversity in the publishing of French books. By deciding on book laws this evening, we are doing, like the major cultural nations in Europe – as Germany, France, Italy and Spain have done before us – to give literature their own law. They did it not least to ensure their own language. A small nation with a small language like the Norwegian language should at least do so. It sets out the framework for how to develop the language and create the literature in the future. The bill is inspired by book laws in other European countries. It has many common features with these, but it is not a blueprint, it is adapted to Norwegian circumstances.’ The alternative to today's book law and fixed price is free price. With that is the number of releases, among others. at the Publications Office of the European Communities, reduced by one-third. The situation is the opposite in Norway, where we have a fixed price system — in sharp contrast to our neighbouring countries. People in Norway read and buy more books than ever. The number of titles issued has increased in recent years — between 2005 and 2010 by 14 pst. The only guarantee that the Norwegian boxing success can continue is that we still get a red-green government. The Progress Party is against a book law, against author grants, against purchasing schemes. What the other parts are for, we're a little bit uncertain about. Whether they're for fixed-price systems or not, we're not sure. But at least what we're sure of is that the only guarantee that we get a good literature policy in the years to come is that we keep a red-green government after the harvest.

It is also very important for us that the fixing of discounts between dealer and supplier is based on free negotiations. ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e3608	The placing on the market of books is a market, but the regulation of this market is more than an industrial policy. It's literature policy, it's cultural policy, it's Norwegian language management, and it's Norwegian identity management. The Christian People's Party believes that the fixed price promotes literary breadth, and that the fixed price scheme is an important cultural policy instrument for the development of Norwegian culture and the strengthening of Norwegian languages in a situation of global diversity. The Christian People's Party's conclusion is therefore very clear. We are in favour of a fixed price policy as a key element of Norwegian literature policy. At least we as authorities shouldn't make regulations that make you get away with it. The book industry – the entire literature field – is in a comprehensive transformation. The Christian People's Party is enough of the belief that an industry agreement provides a greater opportunity to ensure a dynamic that enables us to stimulate the transition in line with the challenges of time. That is why the Christian People's Party has also chosen – instead of supporting the proposed book law – to go in to continue the scheme of an industry agreement even after 2014. We believe that in the context of such a renegotiation, attention should be paid to the development of good Norwegian concepts for the digital circulation of books with a view to reaching a broad audience and to making it available in a consumer-friendly way. We are concerned that the ownership of the industry should not appear to be exclusive to the opportunities of the small publishers in the future. On this basis, we can draw two important conclusions: the main decision is that we are on the fixed price system as a key element of literature policy, and in the case of the Christian People's Party, we believe that it is best ensured through a continued agreement rather than a book law.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e3634	Today, Norway is taking the same steps as other great cultural nations have taken before us. For almost 50 years, literature has been regulated by an exception to competition law. Now is the time to raise the literature up and into a law of your own. Literature contributes to the preservation and development of values on which our entire democracy is based, namely freedom of expression, the transfiguration of words, the lack of meaning and the possibility of deepening us in our own value or in an entirely different value. Producing a book isn't like producing a bike, jogging shoes or donger ribukse. Books build democracy, they strengthen the transfiguration of words – behind each book there is a unique creative work. The law we are dealing with today is for the authors and for the readers. The law hangs on four pillars. The law makes it mandatory to have a fixed price for new releases until 1 May of the following year. And the Progress Party says that it means that one doesn't want to have competition. Well, one wants to have competition, but one wants new books to compete on quality and content rather than on price. We suggest a margin adjustment between publishers and bookstores with a discount of 50 pst, so that it's not just the market power that's available. In addition to fiction and sacprosa, the law covers both trade books and e-books. The fourth pillar is a series of decisions about more equal conditions of competition, so that smaller publishers or bookshops are not pushed by players with a stronger market position than they have. I have noticed that an almost collective committee supports the government's literature policy objectives on disability, quality and accessibility, but that there are shared views on how to achieve these objectives. A lot of the debate on the book law has been about how to meet new digital challenges. The criticism of the law includes: that a book law will prevent innovation and development – not least for e-books, and that it will freeze the situation as it is today. We don't think that's going to be the case. New technologies and digital platforms will provide great opportunities for the dissemination of literature as well. There is also something that the law provides. Today, you can read an interview in the Evening Post with one of the people behind it called eReadz, which can become a Norwegian book stream service. He says there: ‘The law is written with a view to letting actors to whom I have access.’ He has been tired so far that several publishers have not wanted to make an agreement with him. The proposal for book law requires all publishers to also deliver books to new independent operators. This means that universal service guarantees that operators like e.g. eReadz, who wants to launch a new power service – actors who want to implement new technologies and be innovative – should be able to do so effectively and be able to compete effectively. The book law also offers new possibilities for creating modern price models as streaming and subscription services for books on the Internet become common. It wasn't as easy to do before. The fixed price scheme also provides economic space for one to invest in technology and innovation. This means, in sum, that the Book Act provides the best framework conditions to ensure the development of a Norwegian e-book market. There is also an independent responsibility on the literature market actors to make use of these possibilities. I will follow e-book developments closely, and within three years I will also carry out an evaluation. I'm very proud that we in Norway now take this important historical step. We assure the authors of their terms and conditions; we provide readers with information on the width, quality and availability of literature. We legislate literature for its democratic function. So we give the culture and the literature a boost.
