ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e3843	Sense if there is a unanimous vote, I would like to add some comments to the matter, because such conflicts that lead to such strong work disputes, it is important to discuss in this Chamber. The Norwegian model is based on a balance of power between employers and employees, in the same way that it is based on a balance of power between fishermen and the purchaser of fish, between a farmer and the processor of raw materials, i.e. the food industry, or in trade. What has made Norway have a decent working life is that we have very much national agreements where rights are granted, but where responsibility is also given. Not least in the case of the conclusion of an agreement, a liability is given. Co-responsibility is given to trade unions, whether they represent sea-going traders or employees, in relation to the contract they enter into. It has been up through our history to have national agreements, because it is through the national agreements that we ensure those who are the weakest in professional and business life. What is the matter in this setting is that Atea is the company that bought up Umoe ICT in 2010, and as they saw it, an agreement was reached – also with the blessing of the EL & IT Association – that Umoe employees would not require a collective agreement, when they had a local agreement from 2011. This relationship is something that the EL & IT Association contests and for which they were given the right to work, and a unanimous judgment stated that the link could strike if necessary in order to reach a collective agreement. The mediation between the parties did not take place. The company Atea has ICT agreements with NTNU and many municipalities. So it's a central, important and powerful enterprise. What is the matter is the question of the extent to which one should contribute to central agreements, and the company Atea is not particularly pleased that one has collective agreements. That's why this conflict happened. As a result, the government had to intervene with compulsory pay, and that is what we have to decide. There is a unanimous committee in favour. But I just want to say that when the EL & IT Association had struck for 18 days, sympathies were announced in a large monn. There are many trade union leaders who will point out that in the history of labour law, there is a well-known way to ensure that the government intervenes – and that labour law decides the case – that an extension to a sympathetic striker. In this case, we are hoped that the national payroll department that is now set to decide on the case will deal with it so that one places emphasis on having a working life that ensures that there are rights and obligations, and that there are country-wide agreements, which ensure more comprehensive assessments than what local agreements provide.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e3879	As rapporteur of the case, I point out that the amendments to this bill essentially relate to the follow-up to matters previously dealt with in Stortinget, and/or clarifications of the applicable law. The first four proposals concern: the obligation to assess the benefits of training before deciding on special education replaces the half-yearly report on special education with one annual report obligation for schools to cooperate in the preparation and follow-up of measures and objectives at individual level of use of school staff who are not employed in the teaching position and who are to assist in the training. All of this is a follow-up to the Report. St. 18 for 2010–2011, Learning and community, which is about early work and good learning environments for children, young people and adults with special needs. The background to the next two proposals – the provision of compensation with a shared burden of proof for psychosocial issues and the extension of the limitation period in criminal proceedings on psychosocial environments – is a 2011 study on legal instruments to combat bullying carried out at the University of Oslo on behalf of the Ministry of Knowledge. The psychosocial environment was also widely discussed in the report. St. 20 for 2012–2013, which Stortinget debated last Friday, 14 June. The other proposals — permission to adopt a regulation on the common school system, permission to adopt a regulation on a ratio between the number of pupils and the number of teachers, clarification of the concept of ‘recognised educational orientation’ in the provisions of the Private School Act on the recognition of schools and technical change in the second paragraph of Section 13-10 of the Training Act — are individual proposals with different backgrounds. As rapporteur of the case, I am pleased that the Committee endorses the proposals for changes to the training law that were just mentioned. I also refer to a proposal for a change to Section 2-1 of the Private School Act, which means that the approval of offering secondary education on the basis of Montessori pedagogics is cancelled. The proposal is mentioned in Chapter 11 of the Proposition. The Committee has not been able to give sound consideration to Chapter 11 of the proposal before the Great Britain splits up before the summer. Therefore, the Committee has not dealt with the proposal of the proposal for amending the private school law under this chapter in substance. The Committee therefore agrees that the draft legislative amendment on this point is not addressed to voting in the Stortinget. Thus, the proposal for amending the private school law § 2-1, second paragraph, point (b) is not included in the draft decision of the suspension. Similarly, the proposal for entry into force in the second paragraph of III is not included in the draft decision of the vote.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e3909	As the rapporteur has explained, many of these legislative proposals have been discussed in detail in previous debates in the Stortinget. So I'm going to try to be brief. There is agreement on a lot, among other things. to strengthen the legal home of the fight against bullying school students. Going from six-monthly report to one annual report in the PP service, we agree. This allows those working on this to spend more time on children and less time on paper – that's good. Then I can draw the attention of both listeners and readers to the budget debate for the current year, when we discussed the red-green proposals for a legal standard of teacher density. At the time, we agreed that there was a gap in knowledge on this issue. We also all know that there is a great shortage of teachers out there, and that a standard of teaching density is not likely to lead to more teachers in school, but to more unskilled students in school. The area where we don't have a lot of knowledge is the quality of the teacher's staff – the importance it has for students' learning outcomes. That is why the Progress Party today is not willing to vote for what is as § 8-3, because we know too little to say that it will improve the quality of the school. But now, that is, before the grant scheme has entered into force in school and despite the fact that the Ministry repeats in the bill that the knowledge base is uncertain, the Department asks for the KS to refer to it as a blank authority to be able to introduce a teaching norm. ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e4166	It is not often this happens, but in this case it is necessary. The majority of the Committee, the Party of Advances, the Right and the Christian People's Party, requests that Chapter 4 of the Proposition be returned to the Government. The background for this is that the case proposal is very thin. This in-depth reorganization of Norwegian special forces is dedicated to just a few sentences in the proposition. In such an important case, the size must be presented with a decision-making basis in which the consequences, cost and assumptions are made visible and verifiable. In the very brief dossier, there is a lack of background, premise and cost and impact assessments. Unfortunately, there are several cases in which the Ministry of Defence delivers cases to the Stortinget which impair the Storting's ability to make good decisions and which impair the legitimacy of defence policy. It's very unfortunate, critical and not an acceptable way to present matters. The size depends on a sound decision-making basis when deciding on acquisitions in the billion class or making decisions with major operational and organisational implications. We experienced the same thing in connection with Prop. 110 S for 2010–2011, where Stortinget was asked to decide on the purchase of four training aeroplanes of type F-35. It was also the largest acquisition of Norway's history that we started at that time, and it was treated over two columns in the proposition. When we discussed the long-term defence plan last spring, we experienced the same thing. The Committee asked 291 written questions to clarify the matter. In both cases, we also called the Minister of Defence and the Chief of Defence to the hearing. Such a practice with a lack of decision-making basis for the Storting is only possible because the government can push matters through, with the majority of the government parties in the hall. This time, too, the Committee has tried to remedy the lack of information by asking questions and organising consultations, but the majority therefore consider that the matter is not well explained. The unrest and controversy which, in addition, seemed to characterise the internal process leading up to the government's proposal is an additional element which is not suitable for providing confidence that all the premises and consequences of the case have been highlighted. Against this background, the majority believe that the government needs to come back to the Storting with a new case relating to the issue of the organisation of the special forces, based on measures that actually increase operational capacity, and where the premises, costs and consequences have been explained in a sufficiently good and verifiable way. I would also like to add that, on the right, we support the Government's proposal No. 3 in the setting.

The Government is currently authorised to order six F-35 fighter aircraft – with additional equipment and services required – with expected delivery in 2017. The cost of that part of the acquisition is SEK 12.9 billion. kr, of which SEK 4.5 billion. kr constitute the six aeroplanes, while the rest of the costs are distributed between the necessary integration work, the acquisition of a simulator, ICT and logistics and education activities. The costs of the Norwegian fighter aircraft acquisition lie within the estimates set out in the State budget. The expected total cost of acquiring 52 aeroplanes with ancillary equipment is 62,6 billion. 2013-kronor. Others from the Workers' Party will take into account the very important industrial programme in the acquisition of fighter aircraft – which is also widely agreed in the Stortinget. So over to today's battle theme: the organisation of our special forces. In Norway, we have two Special Force Divisions: the Special Command of the Defence Army – located at Rena in Østerdalen and Vealös at Horten – and the Marine Hunter Command – located at Haakonsvern at Bergen and Ramsund in Ofoten. This is the best thing we have of defense departments in Norway, and the soldiers there do a very good and important job of which there is great respect. But we have to be honest and say that the departments are of limited size. That is why we want two relatively small departments to work together to a greater extent – and to pull alternates. For this reason, joint management of the two departments is now being established – directly under the direction of the head of defence. So there will be a status increase of the special forces both organisationally and in fact. I'm saying it even more precisely: with what we're doing now, we're strengthening the special forces. This really shouldn't be to misunderstand. It shall consist of: The Marine Hunter Command shall be put on national alert to assist the police in the resolution of counter-terrorism tasks – as is the Special Command of the Defence/Shunter Command of the Army. A joint strategic management is established to ensure better interaction between the two Special Forces departments. And – as mentioned earlier: New building mass shall be built for the special forces at both Haakonsvermn and Rena. There have been some media speculations about the future organisation of the special forces. I would like to stress once again that the two Special Forces departments are still to be located as today, at Rena and Vealos and Haakonsvern and Ramsund respectively. In addition, the departments shall maintain their close links with their respective defence branches – the Army and the Maritime Defence. At the same time, the proposal requires the selection, basic training and basic training of officers and officers to continue to take place in the two Special Forces departments – as at present. The establishment of a joint management of the special forces facilitates the joint development of the special forces and better interaction between the tactical units – in areas such as preparedness, training and exercises. In addition, a joint special strength management will also enhance the capacity for overall priority and resource use. These are measures to improve the visibility of the importance of and increase the status of our special forces. Let me say that again: we are increasing the status of the special forces and raising them up in the organization – right under the top management. So to what I should say, it is not to avoid that defence policy often becomes district policy, that the debate often goes high, that there are many strong opinions. And that's good. However, it is also important to emphasise the following two points: Norway does not spend large sums on defence to keep industry alive, and Norway does not spend large sums on defence to employ. The money we spend on the defense is to be spent on the defense of the country. The defence is, as we all talked about on Friday when we adopted gender-neutral protection obligations, the sharpest instrument our democracy has. It is particularly important that democracy adopts both the framework and the organisation of our defence. It is from decisions taken in this Chamber and from the duty of protection that the defence is given its legitimacy. I'm going to admit one thing, and that's that I'm on my neck is a Nordic. There can't be any surprise at anyone in this room, I'm an SV and a supporter of strong Nordic defence cooperation and a break with NATO's out of area policy. I represent the only party on the Stortinget that stands for this policy, despite the fact that we agree with our business partners on the basis on which we are now governed. And I think that's okay. Therefore, it is probably no surprise that, in the last period when I did not sit on the Stortinget, I would rather have seen that it was Swedish aircraft we bought to meet our need for an air defence. SV is a follower of a good Norwegian fighter aircraft, and I believe that our safety and our interests could well have been taken care of by Swedish aircraft, with the cooperation with Sweden such a purchase would also be given. So the government lands on F-35. And then it has been important for SV to ensure that the acquisition of combat aircraft does not come out of control, either cost or time. Too many share Ellingen's concerns. That is why both we and the other red-green parties have been concerned that the defence had to take a greater share of the acquisition costs than it was originally foreseen, that a clear cost framework has been set for the purchase of combat aircraft, and that the Storting should deal with each order made, so that there is real parliamentary control. I'm very happy that we've had a majority on it. I am glad that the Minister of Defence was so clear on this at the hearing. SV believes it is a right decision to strengthen counter-terrorism capabilities by putting the Marine Hunter Command on standby for enforcement assistance to the police. A lot of people might think this is high time. The very idea of enforcement assistance challenges our traditional and very important distinction between the responsibilities of the police and the defence, but at the same time it is important to use the resources we have in Norway well, and for my own part I must add: to avoid unnecessary militarisation and arming of the police. The defence is based on — no matter what is done in the police — competences and capacities that the police, for natural reasons, may not have to the same extent. I would like to refer to exciting contributions from both Stein Ørnhøi and Kåre Willoch during the last year of the social debate that deserves debate – also here in the Stortinget. We will also come back to that later when the government submits its informed consultation proposals for legislation that regulates this. But back to the case: I think it's a good solution that FSK and MJK continue as two departments, and retain their different selections and educations, while establishing a joint management of the special forces. It's not a combination – I just have to stress it, because it was said earlier. Norway is a small country, and the availability of equipment and aircraft, vehicles and good practice partners is limited, and there will be a great advantage in seeing the two forces in context and making overall priorities. In my view, this also means a status increase for the special forces with which both MJK and FSK/HJK should be satisfied.

The size must be informed of any deviations.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-17.ana.ud2592e4563	The modernisation of defence is one of the most comprehensive and successful changes that the public sector has undergone in Norway. We're going to continue this process right now. The government has through Prop. 136 S submitted proposals for the approval of seven new projects in the field of property, construction and construction and proposals to extend the order authorisation for the fighter aircraft programme to acquire six F-35 fighter aircraft. Furthermore, the proposal contains priority actions for the further development of the Special Forces of Defence. We are now tabling proposals on the approval of investments in properties, construction and installations for a total of SEK 1.6 billion. kr. At the same time, we propose to extend the order authorisation for the fighter aircraft programme to a total of 12.9 billion. kr. Of course, these are good reasons. These investments will significantly increase our ability to control our own air, land and sea territory. Investments also contribute to the realisation of the defence structure adopted by the Stortinge in examining the current long-term defence plan. I am pleased that the Committee joins in the proposals for new construction projects and at the same time gives its approval to the commissioning authority for the fighter aircraft programme. So to the investments involved: the government will facilitate a compact collection of defence activities at Bardufoss in Rusta camp. In addition, the government is proposing to provide the defense's main helicopter base at Bardufos' future-oriented facilities to solve their mission. At Haakonsvern, the government will ensure good framework conditions for cooperation between the Maritime Defence frigates and the NH90 helicopters and will therefore start the construction of helicopter exposure. The Marine Hunter Command shall have appropriate facilities to maintain and further develop the high quality of Norwegian Special Forces. The Rena construction project will provide special forces with facilities to prepare the loads that can be dropped from aircraft and ensure the proper management of the material. In Bodø, damage and defects are recorded on the main roller coaster. In order to ensure both military and civil aviation activity, the government has found it necessary to carry out a runway upgrade. The take-up of the main acquisition of fighter aeroplanes constitutes, for cost-effective purposes, the majority of the actions promoted in the proposition. It is also one of the most important investments in the defence of Norway in the foreseeable future. F-35 will replace our old F-16 and form the backbone of Air Force and Defense for the next 40 years. It has been asked by the representative Eriksen Soreide about the position an acceleration of the order gives us. It gives us a better position for industrial agreements and to be part of the programme as an active and credible partner. We have recently received feedback on this through the meeting of the fighter aircraft program last week in the United States. It is also the case that the acceleration of investment will make it easier to phase out F-16 and phase in F-35, and we will have better control over both the training of personnel and the demanding operation that must then take place. The size is well known that F-35 is a fifth-generation combat aircraft with unique capabilities, and that F-35 will be a hub in future network-based operations. The first six aircraft in the main acquisition will be scheduled for Norway in 2017. Total costs lie within the cost picture previously reported in the Stortinget and represent 62 billion. 2013-kronor. The supply plan also indicates that the initial operational capability of the new fighter aeroplanes will be in 2019. This is important in order to maintain a satisfactory operational level during the transition phase between F-16 and F-35 and to facilitate proper operational operations. Today, 65 aircraft have been delivered to the partner nations, and the signals I receive about the development of F-35 through the international programme assure me that the new planes will come to Norway in 2017, and that they have sufficient maturity. In the case of industrial agreements, from the day one, the government started working on this – and that was about when we became a government in 2005 – worked for industrial agreements. The development of 25 mm of APEX ammunition at Nammo AS and Joint Strike Missile, JSM, at Kongsberg Group are good examples of Norwegian industry being far ahead in the development of modern technology. These are also important parts of the Norwegian industrial programme. Subject to successful development and integration, both APEX and JSM will be key capacities on Norwegian flights. The development and planned integration of APEX ammunition is on track, and we are experiencing great interest among other nations. Step 2 of JSM development is planned to be completed during the summer. The Ministry of Defence plans to return to Stortinget during the autumn of 2013 with proposals for decisions to implement the next stage of development. Since 22 July 2011, it has been important for the government to further develop the ability of the defence sector to assist civil society in crisis situations. Special forces are here a strategic resource. Over time, the defence has developed very high-quality specialised forces — many of whom have been involved — with very relevant and requested capacities. What we are proposing in this proposition is that the Marine Hunters' Command should be put on the alert to assist the police in resolving counter-terrorism tasks and that a common area of activity should be established to ensure better coordination between the two Special Forces Departments of Defence. The emergency mission is based on the maritime special competence and capacity of the Marine Hunter Command and will also cover enforcement assistance to the police in rural areas. This preparedness shall be maintained from Bergen. In addition to this, the preparedness of the Special Command of the Defence Force/the Army's hunter command comes from Rena. I have to say that I am a little surprised when so much more documentation is requested regarding this. It's a little bit unclear what's going on. In the investment proposition, we have explained large investment costs, in particular Haakonsvern and Rena for the special forces, and we have also answered questions where we say that this year an additional supplement in the readiness for the Marine Hunter Command will amount to 3.4 million. kr. We also say in the investment proposition that we could possibly come back to this in the Maritime Defense budget, and we'll come back to it in the budgetary process. The common area of activity is important, and I would like to stress that this does not involve the merging of the tactical departments, which I have actually heard a representative say here today. It's not a merger, it's a joint management. It is very important that these two departments should be able to operate both their own selection and training of their own personnel and develop their own nature, but joint management is important for coordination and – as the representative Myrli said – as a direct priority of the special forces and as an increase in their level. So for an information issue: The size has been previously informed about a total planning framework of 129 million. kr to meet the total need for security measures at the Armed Forces Operational Headquarters. After the completion of tendering procedures, the overall planning framework for the project is likely to have to be adjusted. In order to ensure the fastest possible start-up and progress, the Ministry of Defence intends to approve the increased cost framework for the action. Detailed quality assurance of other planned measures is necessary and the Government will return to the Storting in an appropriate manner in relation to this case. In conclusion, I would like to say that I am pleased to be able to record that there is a broad majority in the Committee and in the Stortinget for the implementation of both EBA projects and the order authorisation of the new fighter aircraft. This will provide the Defense with a significantly enhanced capability. Further development of the Special Forces of Defence will both enhance operational capability and strengthen preparedness.

This is also how the government, in my opinion, follows good advice from both professionals and NATO, which, in its 2008 study – NATO Special Operations Forces Study – points out the crucial importance of a clear and clear management anchored at the strategic level. There are recommendations we have seen in several areas regarding this type of activity. It provides a better basis for optimal coordination and resource use, which we absolutely need. The maintenance of the location, with joint management, facilitates the joint development of the forces and synergies between the forces in terms of training, exercises and preparedness. This is done at the same time as the forces can maintain their close links with their respective sectors of defence, and the selection, basic training and basic training of officers and officers will continue to take place in the departments, as at present. Nevertheless, joint management will – more than today – set common professional standards and develop common techniques and procedures. This will also ensure, as the Ministry says in its response to the Committee, ‘greater flexibility in the use of forces’. Such flexibility is necessary with such a limited resource as the special forces are in fact, and will give us greater operational capability. After all, it's the most important thing. Moreover, it is gratifying that today we will also adopt a strengthening of the location and storage capacity of the special forces, among other things. Located on the river Rena in the Østerdalen. There will be room for rigging, packing and drying cargo, as well as storage capacity for droplets and parachutes. These are also measures that help to strengthen the capacity we are talking about. The investment in Haakonsvern has the same argument — once again a good example of the fact that it is essential for the government parties to constantly increase and strengthen the capacity and capacity of the forces. All in all, this is therefore a day when the special forces come out strengthened, and we welcome this.

Our defence is the whole defence of Norway. It's one of the most important and heaviest questions we can deal with in Stortinget, and it's different in nature from most of the other policies we work with. What we are discussing tonight are investments to safeguard our defence capabilities at a time when our neighbourhoods, for example. increases in strategic importance and will do so in the foreseeable future. We have an interest in maintaining a defunct, peaceful and primarily civil sovereignty in the north with the ability to defend ourselves in the back. How we then dimension and organize our defence is a matter that requires a lot of thorough debate, and the decisions we take must be in line with our security policy interests. The fact that our decision base is a mosaic of different motivations and is influenced by which states we represent, and which parties we sit here for, doesn't change that. A foreigner who is in the territory or at the Norwegian border shall, upon application, be allowed a stay if, without falling within the first subparagraph, the foreigner is in real danger of being subjected to the death penalty, torture or other inhuman or degrading treatment or punishment upon return to his country of origin. As a general rule, the applicant shall be recognised as a refugee after the first subparagraph even when the need for protection has arisen after the applicant has left the country of origin and is due to the applicant’s own actions. In assessing whether exceptions to the general rule are to be made, particular attention shall be paid to whether the need for protection is due to offences punishable by Norwegian law or whether it appears most likely that the main purpose of the acts was to obtain a residence permit. In such cases, the decision may be valid for up to 10 years. The first subparagraph shall apply mutatis mutandis if the host family intentionally or grossly negligently infringes obligations laid down in or under the Tax Payments Act § 5-4, first and fourth paragraphs.
